#3594. Amy Poehler Leads Young Women Archaeologists Who Make An Important Discovery In Peacock’s ‘Dig’ Trailer

Peacock has unearthed the first trailer for its comedy Dig starring Amy Poehler and co-created by Michael Schur (The Good Place, Parks and Recreation). The 10-episode comedy series premieres on the streamer November 23.

“Everyone, listen up, because I’m only going to explain this once,” Poehler’s Elise tells her young field crew. “There are stray dogs everywhere. Do not feed the dogs, ever.”

Several of the troops on the archaeological site try to fight her command, but she cuts them off every time, no matter the excuse.

The series, which follows a team of dedicated archaeologists and less-dedicated college students spending their summer at the Grecian dig site when they unearth a rare artifact, is an adaptation of Kate Myers’ bestseller “Excavations.” Author Kate Myers is a co-executive producer. Watch on Deadline

Hugh Laurie’s British character Neville chimes in over the intercom to greet the excavation team on the dig site in Greece, where he describes himself as “the face of the archeological dig” but adds that Elise is “its beating heart.”

The male archaeologist then confesses he has some news to share with Elise: his retirement. But she points out that they’ve been down this path before. Sure enough, during what looks to be an announcement to the field crew, Neville pivots and says “Okay, I’ll stay one more year.”

Then, Elise and Geraldine Viswanathan’s character uncover an ornate-looking statuette in the ground, and they have a big choice to make about how to report the discovery. If they tell Neville, “he’ll get the credit like he always does.”

“But if we dig it up ourselves, you’ll get the credit,” Viswanathan says to Elise, who compares the scandal to Watergate, which the younger archaeologist says she’s read when Elise asks if she knows what the reference means.

“I have been wanting to take over this dig for a very long time,” Elise says at the end of the clip. “This may be our only shot.”

The trailer ends with Antonia Thomas, Fina Strazza, Viswanathan and Poehler’s characters maiming themselves for the cause. Pat Benatar’s “Heartbreaker” also backs the clip.

In addition to Laurie, Viswanathan, Thomas and Strazza, Kayvan Novak, Jon Pointing and Bonita Friedericy also appear. The trailer shows glimpses of guest stars Max Greenfield and Jason Mantzoukas. Brennan Lee Mulligan, Noma Dumezweni and Dan Bucatinsky will also guest star.

Dig is co-showrun by co-creator and executive producer Schur and executive producer J.J. Philbin. Poehler also serves as co-creator and executive producer in addition to starring as a series regular.

Still more executive producers include Morgan Sackett, Dean Holland, Kate Arend and Jordan Grief for Paper Kite, Dave Becky and David Miner for 3 Arts, Sharon Jackson for Ocean Avenue. Dig hails from Universal Television, a division of Universal Studio Group, and it is produced by Fremulon, Paper Kite, Duck Duck Films, Dunshire, 3 Arts and Ocean Avenue.

Delaney will be tasked with overseeing digital publishing and NBC News AI efforts, work that has become more important as NBC News builds out a suite of digital and streaming products. #3592. Rosettes and Other Hidden Traces of Hamburg's Tram

Almost 50 years after Hamburg’s last tram ran, I went looking for what it left behind, from rosettes on house walls to rails under the asphalt.

When I was writing about the Fuhle, I came across a small detail: in 1895, the first tram line in Hamburg built for electric operation from the start ran along Fuhlsbüttler Straße. I knew that Hamburg once had trams, but I never knew much about them. Berlin and Munich still have tram networks, so why is Hamburg the largest city in Germany without one?

Digging deeper, I realised the network was much bigger than I thought. In 1954, Hamburg’s trams carried about 251 million passengers. To put that into perspective, all Hochbahn buses together carried 261 million passengers in 2025, and the U-Bahn 291 million. The network was so big that, almost 50 years after the last tram ran in 1978, its traces are still around us.

So I went looking for them. I wanted to put these traces together like pieces of a map, so that when you walk past one of them, you can picture the tram network that once connected the whole city.

Hamburg’s tram story starts with horses. In 1866, the first horse-drawn tram started running from the city centre to Wandsbek, which back then was still a separate town.

In 1894, the first electric tram started running around the old town. The overhead wire was hung from masts on wide streets and fixed to house walls with cast-iron rosettes on narrow ones. Altona, still a separate city then, had its own tram company, with its own design of richly decorated cast-iron masts. Over the following decades, the network grew from Harburg in the south to Schnelsen in the north.

The bombing of 1943 destroyed large parts of it, and some routes never came back. In 1958, the SPD and FDP Senate decided to expand the U-Bahn and phase out the tram. Hamburg was planning for cars, and buses were seen as the modern alternative. Line after line closed, and the rails were often just paved over. On 1 October 1978, the last tram ran from Rathausmarkt to Schnelsen while crowds lined the street to say goodbye.

To see how big the network was, I rebuilt it on today’s map. There was no single source with all the lines, so I combined old network maps from Wikipedia with the work of people who have spent years on this topic, like Horst Buchholz, who wrote a chronicle of every line on his website horstbu.de, and fredriks.de, which has collected the old routes and traces of the tram for more than two decades. The result is a map of the old tram network along with the stations on top of today’s city.

Seeing all the lines together shows how far the tram reached: from Eidelstedt and Lurup in the west to Jenfeld and Billstedt in the east, south towards Harburg, and, of course, along the Fuhle.

The tram has been gone for almost 50 years, but once you know what to look for, you find it on walls, poles, pavements and buildings all over the city.

In narrow streets, there was no space for masts, so the overhead wire was anchored directly to the houses. For this, cast-iron rosettes were fixed to the façades: round, decorated wall plates that held the wire above the street. They were cheaper than masts, and the decoration made them look like part of the building rather than a piece of technical equipment.

While cycling around the city to hunt for rosettes, I quickly realised they are harder to spot than I expected. Some are plain and black, and once you know where to look, they are easy to find. You can find many of them along Ottenser Hauptstraße. Others are painted in the same colour as the façade and blend into its decoration, so you can pass them many times without noticing.

On Hein-Hoyer-Straße in St. Pauli, you can probably see the widest range on a single street, from very plain ones to really fancy ones. I still wonder why. Were the simple ones put up first and replaced over time, or did some building owners pay extra for a nicer design? What we do know is that Hamburg had several tram companies, five different rosette designs are documented, and later rosettes became simpler, until in the end a plain wall hook was enough.

In the old town, there’s one at the corner of Börsenbrücke and Trostbrücke, on the red brick building of the Patriotic Society. The building stands where Hamburg’s old City Hall was, until it was blown up during the Great Fire of 1842 to stop the flames from spreading. A few steps away, on Rathausmarkt, you can even see three pink/white rosettes on a single façade, right above the French café Bouillon.

The rosettes are not listed as monuments. Whether they stay is up to the building owners, and when a house is renovated, they can quietly disappear. A few of the ones I went looking for were already gone.

On wider streets, the wire hung from masts instead. When the trams were gone, many of these masts were simply kept and used for street lighting. The easiest way to recognise them is the lamps: instead of sitting on top of a pole, they hang on wires stretched across the road, exactly where the tram wire used to be.

Luruper Chaussee is a good place to see this, with a long row of old masts still carrying the lights. In the east, you can follow the former Line 8 along Straßburger Straße and the first part of Friedrich-Ebert-Damm by its masts. Further along Friedrich-Ebert-Damm, the city even put up new masts in the same design as the old ones, so not every tram mast you see there is actually from the tram era. Sometimes it is just a single mast left on its own, like the one in front of Horner Landstraße 194.

The most beautiful ones are on Königstraße in Altona. Opposite Lessers Passage, five richly decorated cast-iron masts from 1896 stand between the street trees. They belonged to Altona’s own tram company, they are the last of their kind in Hamburg, and unlike the rosettes, they are protected monuments.

When a line closed, the rails were usually not taken out. To save money, they were just covered with asphalt, and many of them are still under Hamburg’s streets. Every now and then, road works bring them back to the surface, as happened on Rathausstraße next to City Hall in 2023 and on Max-Brauer-Allee in 2024.

In a few places, you can see real rails without any digging. On Straßburger Straße in Dulsberg, a stretch of track is still visible, and in front of Gymnasium Hochrad in Othmarschen, a few metres of rails filled with bitumen lie in the pedestrian crossing.

Even where the rails are gone, the cobblestones sometimes remember them. On Bei der Reitbahn in Ottensen, the stones that replaced the tracks were laid in a slightly different pattern, so you can still follow the curve of the old line in the street.

I find it fascinating to follow these lines, because it makes me imagine how these streets would look with a tram running through them: probably busier with people, and with fewer cars.

When I first came to Hamburg for an internship, I often took bus line 5, and I was surprised that it drove in its own lanes in the middle of the street. Only when I learnt about the tram did it start to make sense.

After Line 2 closed in 1978, the tracks between Grindelberg, Hoheluftchaussee and Lokstedter Steindamm were removed, and bus lanes were laid in the old track bed in the middle of the road. So along this stretch, the 5 follows the path of Hamburg’s last tram. At some junctions, the bus lanes also follow the wide curves of the old tracks, which were designed for trams, not buses.

The oldest trace of all is in Wandsbek. The depot on Wendemuthstraße was built in 1866 for the very first horse tram, with stables for the horses. Today, it is a bus depot.

Many of the later depots found new lives as shops. The depot in Lokstedt is now a hardware store, the one at Krohnskamp in Winterhude a supermarket, and the old depot on Max-Brauer-Allee in Altona is a supermarket too. Near Ohlsdorf Cemetery, the small terminus building where the trams once turned is now a private art studio.

The biggest site was the Falkenried works in Hoheluft, where most of Hamburg’s trams were built. The workshops closed in 1999, and the area was turned into a residential neighbourhood that kept parts of the old industrial halls. Two of its streets are called Straßenbahnring and Straßenbahnstieg.

Some of the trams themselves have survived too, often in unexpected places. One stands at the hardware store in Lokstedt, another in front of the supermarket at Krohnskamp. If you want to see Hamburg trams in action, the museum at Schönberger Strand near Kiel has the largest collection, and some of them still run on its museum line.

A few travelled much further. Hamburg trams ended up in museums in Denmark and even in the USA: car 2710, built at Falkenried in 1921, now stands in the Seashore Trolley Museum in Maine.

Since 1978, the tram has almost returned twice, and both times politics decided its fate. In 1989, the SPD and the Greens started planning a modern Stadtbahn, but after they lost the 2001 election, the new CDU-led government stopped it. In 2008, the CDU and the Greens brought the idea back, until the Greens left the coalition in 2010 and the plans were put on hold again. A few months later, the SPD won the election with a promise to stop the Stadtbahn, and in 2011 it was officially cancelled.

Today, Hamburg is building the U5 instead, a new U-Bahn line from Lurup to Osdorf, planned to be finished by 2040 at an estimated cost of up to 16,5 billion euros. That keeps the tram in the debate: a recent study suggests a tram network could cover much of the same route for around 1,5 billion euros, while the Hochbahn argues a tram could never carry as many passengers.

Personally, I have a love-hate relationship with trams. I really like them as a form of public transport, especially compared to buses. On the other hand, the wires and the noise feel like a kind of pollution of their own. I can still hear the trams in Lisbon; they were so loud. Buses are slowly becoming electric, but I still don’t find riding a bus a delightful experience.

While writing this, I was curious whether trams have moved on, and it turns out they have. In Nanjing, battery-powered trams run without overhead wires on most of their lines, and Birmingham chose battery trams so it wouldn’t have to fix cables to its historic Town Hall, a modern answer to the same problem Hamburg once solved with rosettes.

If Hamburg ever brings the tram back, I hope it will be a quiet one without wires, maybe even floating silently like a maglev train. Until then, I’ll keep looking up at the rosettes and down at the cobblestones, imagining the network that once ran through the city.

How one architect’s ideas from a hundred years ago still shape the way we live, walk and cycle through Hamburg today.

From number 1 to 792, I take you along Fuhlsbüttler Straße to show you why it deserves a place among Hamburg’s famous streets.

A public Kanban board could make Hamburg’s projects more transparent and strengthen democracy by showing citizens how public money is being spent.

A walking tour led me into the story of Altona, the Danish-ruled city that grew beside Hamburg by offering freedoms its powerful neighbour did not.

A local's take on Hamburg city life, from local politics to everyday moments, new discoveries, hidden gems, and the latest from the Franzbrötchen app.

#3591. ‘Loser Core,’ ‘EBK,’ and Other Gen Z and Gen Alpha Slang You Might Need Help Decoding

If constantly being mogged by sigmas has you feeling like a fuhuhluhtoogan, and you have no idea what any of that means, you've come to the right place. This glossary aims to define and explain popular slang words and phrases of Generation Z (usually defined as people born between 1997 and 2012) and Generation A (anyone born between 2012 and now) so you'll know what the hell people are talking about.

A word of warning: If you aren't of those generations, and/or you need an online list to know what slang words mean, you should not say them aloud unless you're trying to be embarrassing.

Ballhog: A fashion trend among Gen Z and Gen A characterized by wearing vintage clothes that look normal, even drab, to most, but that people in the know can recognize as interesting or unique retro clothing.

Benny tripping: Intentionally overdosing on Benadryl to experience an (almost always unpleasant) "trip."

Crunged: The past tense of "cringe," "crunged" is often used to describe extreme cringe, like "I cringed so hard I crunged."

EBK: stands for "Extreme Baller Knowledge." It goes beyond the sports reference to apply to to anyone who has deep knowledge of a subject. It's usually used ironically.

GiGis: Good game. Pronounced like the name Gigi. The post game pleasantry "good game" has been a staple in sports since forever. Gamers adopted it and shortened it to "GG" in text. So GiGis is a way of saying "good game."

Glass skin: Describes skin that is so smooth, luminous, and clear, it seems to shine.

Loser core: A youth sub-culture/musical sub-genre categorized by listening to depressing, alternative-inspired music and dressing in drab, oversize clothing.

304: Hoe. (Type "304" on a calculator and turn it upside down.)

4+4: Ate. Four plus four is eight, or "ate." (See "ate.")

6-7 (or 67): This piece of Generation Alpha brainrot slang doesn't mean anything. It's just funny to some people to say "6-7," especially in answer to any question involving numbers. Ex: "Q: What time is it? A: six-seveeen."

6-7 weekend: A weekend in which Saturday falls on the 6th day of the month and Sunday on the 7th.

7x7=49: Often refers to a man who is attractive in a self-evident way. Based on the belief that the equation 7x7=49 makes intuitive sense in a way other equations do not.

80/20 rule: An axiom in online incel spaces, the 80/20 is the idea that 80% of women only date the "top" 20% of men.

Algo speak: Coded language used to bypass online content moderation. Examples: "regarded" used instead of "retarded" and "unalive" instead of "kill."

Alpha male: Taken from animal ethology, an alpha male is the dominant member of a group of males, or just a male who is in charge. (See "beta male" and "sigma male.")

Alpine divorce: An alpine divorce is when a man takes his wife or girlfriend on a hike, usually in the mountains, and then just leaves her there to fend for herself.

ASAP (or A$AP): When a rapper has ASAP or A$AP in front of their name, it indicates affiliation with the ASAP Mob, a New York hip-hop collective.

Ate/eat: Done very well, often regarding clothing. e.g.: "You ate that outfit." See also: "serving."

Aura: Someone who is mysterious and cool is said to "have aura."

Aura battle: Aura is coolness or charisma ("rizz" if you prefer). An "aura battle" is a public contest in which the person with the most aura, as determined by the crowd and/or comment section reaction, wins. (See "Aura")

Aura farming: Depending on the context, “aura farming” can refer to a person who does something cool without trying or someone who is trying too hard to appear cool.

Baby Boo syndrome: A joking reference to people who are very into the Baby Boo memes popular on TikTok.

Baddie: A bad/wild girl. Meant as a compliment.

Baka: Japanese word meaning “crazy" or “foolish.” Used mainly in the anime community.

Bed-rotting: Staying in bed all day. You may know it as “lazing around.” (See "Hurkle-durkle.")

Beez: Popularized in Nicki Minaj's 2012 track "Beez in the Trap," beez means something like "I am always." So "beez in the trap" means "I am always in the trap." (See "Trap.")

Beta male: A beta male, or just "beta," is a weaker, subservient male. (See "alpha male" and "sigma male.")

Boombayah: A euphemism for having sex. It’s used mostly online, often to defeat censorship algorithms.

Bop: A girl who sleeps around. Also: a great song.

Boy aquarium: TikTok slang that describes hockey games.

Boy kibble: The male response to "girl dinner," boy kibble is food boys eat. It usually consists of rice and ground beef, often served without spices or vegetables.

Boysober: Someone who has sworn off sex, relationships, and/or dating.

Brain-rot: A description of the overuse of stupid slang. See also: “Skibidi.” Also used to describe the effects of being overly online.

Brat: The contemporary meaning of "brat" is an adjective describing a person who is edgy, imperfect, and confident. It was coined by pop star Charli XCX who defined it as "that girl who is a little messy and likes to party and maybe says some dumb things sometimes. Who feels herself but maybe also has a breakdown."

Bruzz: Bros. Part of the -uzz family of slang words. See "Huzz" and "-uzz."

Bubba truck: A lifted or otherwise modified pick-up truck.

Catch a fade: To have a fight. (See "fade.")

Chad: An attractive man; an “alpha male.” See "Giga-Chad."

Chat: A reference to streamers addressing their chat windows aloud. Saying “chat” in real life is an ironic joke.

Cheesin': The slang word "cheesin'" refers to smiling, particularly a big, goofy smile. It comes from the common exhortation to "say cheese!" before a photo is taken. The word "cheesing," with a "g" at the end, often means the same thing, but it can also refer to the trend of throwing slices of cheese at cars and/or people.

Chopped: Ugly. Chopped is often applied to someone's face or outfit, but anything that isn't aesthetically pleasing could be called "chopped," as could anything that is generally not good, like an awkward situation.

Choppelganger: A portmanteau of "chopped" and "doppelgänger," a choppelganger is someone who looks like another person, but worse. (See "chopped.")

Chud: A physically unappealing person. Sometimes used for a man who holds right-wing views.

Clanker: A slur aimed at robots and AI agents pretending to be human.

Clock that: "I understand and agree." Not to be confused with older slang, where "clock" meant to see, or even older slang where "clock" meant "to hit or punch."

Corn: “Corn” is algo-speak that means “porn.” Used in online spaces where the word might cause your account to be flagged or banned.

Cortisol-coded: Describes something that's meant to be relaxing but is actually stressful, like watching a video of the 5 a.m. yoga routine of an influencer surrounded by $400 candles.

Coworker-core: A catch-all description for things that are unfunny or uninteresting in a way that appeals to older people.

Cracked: This slang term has two meanings: to have sex, as in "I got cracked last night," and to be really good at something, like "he’s cracked at Fortnite."

Crashout: To have an intense emotional outburst, usually accompanied by impulsive behavior. Crashing out is often due to being overwhelmed or overly frustrated.

D1 Crashout: An epic, elite level, extreme crashout. (See "crashout.)

Dah Bih Gah: "That bitch good," ie: "I like that thing." Usually used to describe food.

Dah Bih Tah: "That bitch tough," ie: "I like that thing." Usually used to describe food.

Dead: Past tense of having died laughing. If someone responds to a joke with "dead" or a skull emoji, they find it funny.

Deadass: Seriously. Used like, "I am deadass not lying."

Deriod: A combination of "dick" and "period," deriod refers to men seeming to experience the mood swings commonly associated with women's menstrual cycles.

Dih: Algo speak for “dick.” (See "Algo speak.")

Doi doi doi: This piece of brain-rot slang doesn't mean anything. It's correctly pronounced by putting your hand up to your mouth and saying "doi doi doi" in a way that sounds funny.

Doomer: A person who is overly negative and/or cynical.

Dwerking: A male-centric variation of twerking. A sexually suggestive dancing characterized by rapid, repeated hip thrusts and shaking of the dick. Other variants include "pwerking" and "bwerking" where the "p" and "b" stand for pussy and boobs.

Edgar: A variation of the Caesar haircut worn especially among Hispanic males. Also refers to the kind of person who wears the haircut.

Fambushing: A combination of "family" and "ambushing," fambushing refers to young people checking where their parents are on location-sharing apps so they can get free food—if you see mom at Chipotle, you ask for a burrito.

Fanum tax: The theft of food between friends. Named for streamer Fanum, known for “taxing” his friends by taking bites of their meals or stealing fries.

Fax, no printer: Telling the truth. Since "fax" and "facts" are pronounced the same, this is a colorful way of saying "facts, no cap."

Fent-fold: A description of the bent-over posture of people nodding on heavy drugs.

Fuhuhluhtoogan: Supposedly from Baltimore slang, this is a nonsense word used so people will ask what it means but never receive an answer. Often paired with "Jittleyang."

Fujoshing: The verb form of the Japanese slang term "fujoshi," fujoshing describes women/girls enjoying media that depicts romantic relationships between men. This word is used almost exclusively in online fandom communities.

Gamer dent: The temporary indentation left on someone’s hair or skin after wearing headphones for too long.

Glaze: To overly praise someone, often insincerely, or with the hope of getting something in return.

Gleek: An older slang term that is gaining prominence lately, gleeking describes squirting saliva from under the tongue.

Glizzy: Hot dog. "Glizzy" was originally slang for Glock or gun, but came to mean hot dog based on the hot dog shape of a Glock's magazine.

Green fn: An interjection one might used when someone does something cool or impressive. Often used ironically.

Goonbait: (See "gooning") Goonbait is, essentially, media that exists to inspiring gooning.

Gooner: A man who goons. (See "gooning.")

Goonette: A woman who goons. (See "gooning.")

Gooning: Extended masturbation without orgasm done for the purpose of entering an altered state of consciousness. (See "gooner," "goonette.")

Gyatt or Gyat: Once an interjection used when seeing someone sexy, like “god-DAMN,” “gyatt” has come to mean “attractive booty.”

Heavy soda: Pop with extra syrup. Some gas station soda machines have settings that control the ratio of syrup to carbonated water. A heavy soda is a soda with the syrup level set very high.

Hewwo: An overly cute way of saying "hello." Usually used online, and often ironically.

HGS: Abbreviation for "home girls" used in comment sections.

Hiplet: Derogatory slang for a woman who has "hip dips," i.e. inward curves on their outer thighs just below the hip bone.

Hozier yell: Named for singer/songwriter Andrew Hozier, a "Hozier yell" refers to the sound one makes when experiencing a peak, climactic, and/or awe-inspiring moment.

HTN: This acronym comes from the online incel community, where fairly good-looking men are described as "high-tier normies" or "HTN." (See "normie.")

Hurkle-durkle: Based on an archaic Scottish word, “hurkle-durkle” means to lounge in bed after it is time to get up. See also: “bed-rotting."

Huzz: -uzz slang for "hoes." See "bruzz" and "-uzz."

"It's giving": Used to convey that something has a specific vibe. Example: "That dude texts you every 10 minutes; it's giving desperate."

"It's so over": The situation is hopeless. The opposite of "we're so back." See also: "Doomer."

IWEL: This comment-section acronym is short for "I wouldn't even lie." Sometimes written as "IWL."

Jelqing: The use of stretching or weights in an attempt to increase penis size.

Jittleyang: Supposedly from Baltimore slang, this is a nonsense word used so people will ask what it means but never receive an answer. See also: "Fuhuhluhtoogan."

JOMO: A play on FOMO (fear of missing out) JOMO is an acronym that stands for “joy of missing out.”

"Learn Chinese": Sports slang directed at failing players. They are in danger of being sent to play in China, so they should "learn Chinese."

Loaded water: Water enhanced with something—a flavor boost, fruit juice, electrolytes, carbonation, and even vitamins or prebiotics. The idea is to increase hydration by making water more palatable.

Looksmaxxing: Maximizing one’s physical attractiveness through personal grooming, working out, and dressing stylishly. See also: “-maxxing.”

🪫 (low battery emoji): The low battery emoji means "I am experiencing emotional depletion, heartbreak, or a general feeling of 'I can't anymore.'"

Lowkenuinely: This mash-up of "low key" and "genuinely" expresses sincerity but in a way that says "let's not make a big deal about it."

Lucky Scoops: Lucky Scoops are a kind of TikTok or Instagram video where buyers purchase a scoop that represents a chance to win merchandise. These videos are very popular with children; critics feel like they are a form of gambling.

Mason (or Mason 6-7 kid): A "Mason 6-7 Kid" or "Mason" is a stereotypical male member of Generation Alpha. Mason kids are known for their love of baseball-inspired fashion (caps and shorts), their "ice cream" haircut, and for repeating brainrot slang like "6-7."

-maxxing: A suffix used with any word to indicate trying to improve. Seeing your friends could be called "friendmaxxing," working out could be called "gymmaxxing," making jokes could be called "jestermaxxing," etc.

Mewing: A facial exercise meant to strengthen the jawline.

Mirror sex: Using a mirror to watch yourself have sex.

Misery girl: A young woman with piercings, a penchant for the darkly dramatic, and highly stylized makeup that makes them appear to have a skinny, pointy nose and huge eyes and lips.

Mog: To be more attractive than someone, usually in an intentional or aggressive way. Example: "I was rizzing up this girl, but he walked in and totally mogged me."

"My 90 in a 30": A song played while driving that inspires speeding.

"My steak is too juicy": The phrase "my steak is too juicy" and other variants like "my lobster is too buttery," are online insults that suggest someone is complaining about something they should be grateful for.

Neurospicy: A different way of saying “neuro-divergent.”

Normie: A person who is considered normal or average. It's sometimes an insult suggesting someone is boring and witless. (see also, "mid.")

NPC: Non-player character. Originally describing video game characters, NPC is now used on the internet to mean people who don't think for themselves.

Opp: Short for "opposition." Someone who is out to get you. An enemy.

Performative male: An insult for young men whose tastes, hobbies, and lifestyle are seen as a performance aimed at obtaining societal approval, especially the approval of young women.

Pheromone maxxing: Trying to influence the behaviors of others through your stink. Supposedly, young men in the "looksmaxxing" community are skipping showers and not washing their clothing in an attempt to make young women like them more. (See "-maxxing").

Phonk: The word phonk can refer to one of two musical sub-genres. It originally referred to throwback '90s hip-hop categorized by slow-tempo "chopped and screwed" production and vintage Memphis rap vocals. It now more commonly describes the heavily distorted, up-tempo EDM used in brainrot videos.

Pink cocaine: Also known as "pink snow," pink cocaine is slang for a powdered drug mixture that usually contains some combination of ketamine, MDMA, meth, opioids, and other substances.

"Press F for respect:" In 2014 video game Call of Duty: Advanced Warfighter, the player attends a funeral and a prompt is given that reads "Press F to pay respects." Now, a singular "F" in a chat window indicates respect. It's usually ironic.

Quiet on the creek: A fishing-inspired way to say "keep it on the down low" or "be cool."

Rawdogging boredom: Rawdogging is "doing something with no safeguards or support," so "rawdogging boredom" is consciously doing nothing.

Regarded: “Regarded” is algo-speak for “retarded."

RegencyCore: A fashion and design aesthetic that springs from the popularity of the fantasy-regency era look of the Netflix show Bridgerton, RegencyCore combines the opulent style of the British Regency era of the early 1800s with fantasy elements like pastel colors and gold accents.

"Reheating your own nachos:" This slang phrase comes from cultural critics in online fandom communities. It refers to performers and/or artists whose new works are seen as trying to recapture what was good about their previous artistic output. It's not always negative; it's possible to successfully reheat your own nachos.

Rizz: As a noun, "rizz" means charisma. As a verb, "rizz" or "rizz up" means attracting someone with your charisma.

Scientology speedrun: Young people running into a Scientology building and acting foolish until they are ejected.

Scorigami: A slang term among football fans for when the final score of an NFL game has never happened before in the league's history. The most recent scorigami was on September 28, 2025, when the Green Bay Packers and Dallas Cowboys tied 40 to 40.

SDIYBT: An acronym for "start digging in your butt, twin," this brainrot phrase doesn't mean anything. Some people just think it's funny to say.

Sendy and “Let’s get sendy": Sendy is an adjective based on the older rock-climbing and extreme sports slang phrase "send it." A shortened version of "ascend it," "send it" is said right before one is about to do something challenging. "Sendy" describes a person who is prone to doing bold things. "Let's get sendy" means something like "Let's get wild." It's been adopted by brainrot fans, so it's often repeated with no meaning meant to be attached.

Serve: Wearing a particularly stylish outfit. See also: "ate."

Serve cunt: To act in a powerfully and unapologetically feminine way; to slay.

Sharking: Looking for people to hook up with.

Side quest: A side quest in a video game is a quest that is not necessary to complete the game's main objective. In slang, a side quest is anything you do in life just for the experience of doing it.

Sigma male: An internet-created male classification, a "sigma male" is as dominant as an alpha male, but is outside the male hierarchy, i.e.: a lone wolf. Originally used seriously, the concept was so widely derided it's now almost always used ironically. (See "Alpha male" and "Beta male.")

Skibidi: Named after “Skibidi Toilet,” a popular series of YouTube videos, “skibidi” itself has no specific meaning, beyond ridiculing the overuse of slang itself. See “brain-rot.”

Skrt: Onomatopoetic word for the sound of tires squealing upon acceleration.

Slopcore: This term describes the countless 100s of millions of cheesy-looking, unsettling, AI-generated images, videos, and songs that have hit the internet since AI was given to the masses a couple of years ago.

Slopper: Insult directed at people who use AI programs too much, people who takes conversations with LLMs too seriously, and people who have basically offloaded their thinking to an algorithm.

Snatched: Very attractive and/or flawlessly styled.

Spawn point: Mother. Based on the spot you start in a video game.

Surf Dracula: A hyper-specific phrase that describes "prestige" television series. The joke is that if there were an older TV show called "Surf Dracula," Dracula would be surfing in every episode, but modern shows would make the whole first season about how Dracula got his surfboard.

Sweat: A person who tries too hard, usually used in reference to video games. The adjective form is "sweaty."

SYBAU: An online acronym that stands for “shut your bitch ass up.”

That's AI: "I don't believe you" or "I doubt it," even when not connected to artificial intelligence.

TLPUR: This supposed acronym section has no meaning. The joke is to tell people it means something like "True Love Pills Until Rope" or "Too Late for Puberty."

Tradwife: Believer in traditional married gender roles.

Trap: Once used to refer to a house where drugs are sold, trap has come to describe both a form of music and any place where one works or hustles.

Treatler (and Treatlerite): "Treatler" and "Treatlerite" are online insults that combine "treat" and "Hitler" to refer to entitled users of services like Doordash or Uber Eats who regard luxury delivery services as a human right, and don't consider the hardships of the people who do the work that makes "private taxis for burritos" possible.

TS: TS originally was AAVE shorthand for "this shit," but it is often used to just mean "this."

Turnt: Excited or intoxicated, or excitedly intoxicated.

Tweaking: In older slang, "tweaking" referred to the nervous and erratic actions of someone on drugs, specifically, speed. Generations A and Z have expanded the definition to refer to any actions that are erratic, or even just mildly energetic, not necessarily related to drug use.

Tylenol baby: After President Trump and Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. announced a scientifically dubious link between the active ingredient in Tylenol and autism, kids have been calling each other "Tylenol babies."

Unc: Short for "uncle," used to describe slightly older people. Example: "The class of 2024 are unc-status to the class of 2028." See "yunc."

-uzz: -uzz slang words use "uzz" at the end of any word, so "bros" becomes "bruzz," "hoes" becomes "huzz," "granny" becomes "gruzz," etc.

"We're so back": Opposite of "it's so over."

Venus tummy: When a woman's belly is a little fat, but not too fat, she is said to have a Venus tummy. Named for ancient Greek statues of Venus and Aphrodite who were a little fleshy.

Viking rap: A musical sub-genre consisting of AI-generated Vikings rapping about longboats, raiding, and other Viking things.

Wojack: The name of a style of internet drawings used to quickly stereotype someone. See this post for a full explanation of the Wojak universe.

Yapping: Describes a presentational style often seen on online streams of talking a lot and/or quickly while not saying anything worthwhile.

You the birthday: A phrase that means "you're awesome" or "you're great" in the way a birthday is great: fun, exciting, extra, etc.

Young ho: "Young ho" is both a reclamation of the word "ho" and an expression of youth-based solidarity. Young women are posting videos of themselves defining what makes them "young hos," noting things like "Getting up at 7:50 to be at work at 8," and air-frying chicken nuggets.

Yunc: Yunc is a variation of "unc." In AAVE, a "yn" is a "young n-word." So "yunc" means something like "young uncle," or a person who may be young but has uncle vibes or unc status.

Zoomer Perm: A curly on top, short on the sides haircut popular among young people.

#3590. ServiceNow and Nvidia launch Arc, a new AI agent for enterprise

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

The two companies are trying to solve the AI agent security conundrum with multiple layers of real-time security checks.

One of the main roadblocks to adopting more autonomous AI systems for companies is adequate security controls, especially after a summer of agent-led breaches. Software company ServiceNow has partnered with Nvidia to address that need by combining AI capabilities with security-first guardrails. Arc, which ServiceNow and Nvidia call “a personal long-running AI agent that can take on everyday work,” is generally available as of Wednesday. The companies originally released the idea for the agent back in May. Arc is available now on Mac, but Windows support is still on the way.

“Arc is governed and secured by the ServiceNow Super Intelligence Control Tower and enforced with Nvidia OpenShell, allowing it to work independently while the enterprise stays in control,” the companies wrote. OpenShell is an open-source tool that controls runtime. The Control Tower is ServiceNow’s governance framework for AI agents, which aims to be a catch-all service that monitors what agents are doing, vets their access, checks for security risks like prompt injection, measures agent performance, and more.

Like many agents built for enterprise use, Arc is designed for daily tasks that rely on productivity tools such as email and Microsoft Teams. Designed to run efficiently on Nvidia’s new RTX Spark devices, it can run locally on an employee’s desktop or within Nvidia OpenShell on its own device. Similar to other agents that work off of accumulated context, ServiceNow and Nvidia said Arc builds memory and skills over time so that its performance continually improves.

Also: Microsoft’s new Nvidia PCs are cool for extreme power users – but I’ll wait for AI PC 3.0

The companies also said Arc is efficient, handing more complex tasks off to frontier models only when necessary and keeping data as local as possible otherwise. ServiceNow and Nvidia also said that Arc “proactively surfaces work and action items” by scanning an employee’s inbox and meeting notes.

But more than any new or highly competitive capabilities overall, what sets Arc apart, according to the companies, is the multi-level security framework surrounding it. ServiceNow and Nvidia said their approach offers more reliable security by “moving enforcement beyond the software layer into the runtime and hardware layers.”

Essentially, while the Arc agent is working on tasks, ServiceNow’s Super Intelligence Control Tower enforces company policies, while Nvidia OpenShell restricts the agent’s behavior where necessary.

“IT defines policies in the Super Intelligence Control Tower and NVIDIA OpenShell can be directed to enforce them at runtime,” the announcement explained. “Users obtain granular control over which systems AI agents can access, APIs they can call, and data they can read.”

AI Passport is another layer of agent security that the two companies announced last week. It holds an enterprise’s set of rules, which the Control Tower sets, about risk tolerance and the services Arc and other agents are allowed to access. That lets Nvidia’s features like OpenShell intervene efficiently if an AI agent takes action it’s not supposed to, the companies explained.

With the other pieces of ServiceNow and Nvidia’s security puzzle, AI Passport functions like a bridge between ServiceNow infrastructure and Nvidia’s checkpoints. The entire system is designed to continuously monitor an agent’s actions and permissions to prevent breaches, unsanctioned actions, and other fallout from using agents.

If the Control Tower tells OpenShell an agent’s passport has been revoked, OpenShell will keep the task the agent was trying to execute contained within a sandbox, where reviewers can audit it later. That interruption is also task-specific, meaning other, approved agent actions can continue without restarting.

“When a passport is revoked in the Super Intelligence Control Tower, Nvidia BlueField can enforce that revocation at machine speed before the AI agent can proceed,” the companies said. “This creates enforcement in silicon designed to prevent overrides from compromised software.”

#3589. My Chemical Romance Will Drop Dallas Cowboys Cheerleader Uniforms to Resolve Lawsuit: Report

The band has reportedly chosen to ditch concert costumes that sparked a trademark lawsuit from the NFL cheerleaders.

My Chemical Romance has reportedly caved under legal pressure from the Dallas Cowboys Cheerleaders and agreed to stop using backup dancers in look-alike uniforms at their concerts.

The NFL cheerleaders sued the legendary emo band last week, claiming it was violating their trademark rights by dressing dancers in similar blue-and-white uniforms to stage a faux firefight as part of a show set in a dystopian American future.

Legal experts told Billboard that My Chemical Romance would likely be protected against those claims by the First Amendment’s protections for free speech. But according to a report Tuesday (Oct. 27) from TMZ, the band wasn’t willing to fight that legal battle.

The outlet says the cheerleaders and the band have reached an agreement in which My Chemical Romance will continue to perform the same cheerleader routine on stage — just not with the dancers dressed quite so similarly to the Cowboys squad.

Neither reps for the band nor a lawyer for the cheerleaders immediately returned requests for comment on Tuesday.

The case, filed in Texas state court by the cheerleaders’ parent company Pro Silver Star, came after My Chemical Romance performed the cheerleader bit during the first two performances of a five-show residency at the Hollywood Bowl for the 20th anniversary of The Black Parade.

When the reported settlement was reached, the cheerleaders had been asking a judge to issue an immediate restraining order barring the band from continuing to use the uniforms, including in the final two shows in Los Angeles this weekend.

“Silver Star is not litigious, and it takes no issue with MCR’s music, its artistic ambitions, or any message defendants seek to convey,” wrote Alex Spiro, the well-known celebrity litigator representing the cheerleaders. “Silver Star does not ask defendants to change their setlist, narrative, politics, choreography, or even their decision to put gun-toting performers on stage.

“Silver Star asks only that those performers not wear the DCC Uniform while doing it,” the company added. “The universe of alternative costume designs is effectively unlimited.”

#3588. Hum Are Hitting the Road in 2027

Veteran Illinois shoegaze band Hum have announced a 2027 tour, their first proper run since 2015. The U.S. treck kicks off in Atlanta in March, stopping in Austin, Philadelphia, New York, Boston, Seattle, and more before concluding in San Francisco in July. All dates will feature support from Kansas City’s the Life and Times, while Chicago post-hardcore band Pelican and Brooklyn trio Spotlights will open on East and West Coast dates, respectively. Check out a full itinerary below.

The shows build upon Hum’s well-received headlining sets earlier this year at traveling shoegaze festival Slide Away, where they shared top billing with Chapterhouse and Nothing. Those dates were Hum’s first shows in nearly seven years, a hiatus that also encompassed the death of original drummer Bryan St. Pere in 2021. Shiner’s Jason Gerken, who joined the group on drums during their 2015 tour and at Slide Away, will return to the kit for the forthcoming dates.

Hum’s Slide Away sets marked the first time the band performed songs from their 2020 album Inlet live. Prior to that release, they hadn’t shared a record since 1997’s Downward Is Heavenward, which followed 1995’s You’d Prefer An Astronaut and their 1993 debut Electra 2000.

#3587. Why Rocket Lab Stock Took Flight on Tuesday

Rocket Lab (RKLB +8.34%) stock took flight on Tuesday, climbing as much as 10%. As of 2:51 p.m. ET, the stock was still up 8.4%.

The catalyst that sent the rocket specialist higher was the latest news about its Neutron rocket.

In a post on X today, Rocket Lab announced that the Neutron rocket's second stage -- which is responsible for delivering payloads once in orbit -- and the accompanying thrust module had departed the factory for the Virginia launch site.

It’s a beautiful day to set sail with the next pieces of Neutron flight hardware to Launch Complex 3. Earlier today we sent Neutron’s Stage 2 and Thrust Module out of the Neutron Factory and onto a barge for transport through Chesapeake Bay to Wallops Island in Virginia. Once it arrives, the Thrust Module will go straight onto the launch mount at LC-3 for check outs and the beginning of wider systems demonstrations including integrated fluids tests and module-to-launch-mount interface checkouts. With no strongback on the launch mount by design, Neutron’s Thrust Module is the rocket’s only physical interface to the launch pad, which this upcoming test campaign enables us to test early before the entire vehicle comes together.

In the wake of this announcement, Cantor Fitzgerald analyst Andres Sheppard reiterated his overweight (buy) rating and $122 price target. That suggests potential gains for investors of 127% compared to Monday's closing price. The analyst expects a Neutron launch in the second quarter of 2027, giving investors the opportunity to profit from the recent sell-off.

Earlier this month, Rocket Lab announced that it would delay Neutron's inaugural launch. The flight, originally scheduled for year-end, was delayed until 2027 after the company discovered a defect in the propellant dome.

Rocket Lab stock has been beset by volatility, exacerbated by the recent IPO of Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.14%) -- aka SpaceX. Ahead of the company's public debut, Rocket Lab shares took flight, gaining 115% during the first five months of 2026, but have since plunged more than 61% (as of this writing).

Given the boom-or-bust nature of spaceflight, volatility is here to stay, especially since we're still in the early stages of the "space race." At 44 times sales, Rocket Lab stock isn't cheap, but it is less expensive than SpaceX, which sells for 61 times sales. Moreover, as the space race matures, Rocket Lab is poised to shoot for the stars.

Danny Vena, CPA has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Rocket Lab. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3586. Fox and Endemol Shine Team to Adapt ‘Fear Factor’ as a Scripted Microdrama Series for My Drama App

The market for vertical media content is expanding at a fast clip and it’s attracting more and more high-profile properties, including the enduring gross-out reality show “Fear Factor.”

Fox and “Fear Factor” owner Endemol Shine North America are teaming to adapt the show for vertical media consumption as a scripted series, which adds a dollop of horror-genre heat to the vertical format. The series will consist of 30 episodes running three minutes apiece. This version of “Fear Factor” will be distributed via Holywater Tech’s popular My Drama microdrama app. The deal comes as part of the larger partnership that Fox Corp. set with Holywater last fall. Related Stories 'Beach Fictions' Review: Utterly Beguiling, Time-Tweaking Korean Romance Shows What a Day a Difference Makes ‘Ray Gunn’ Review: Brad Bird’s Latest Animated Feature Is a Giddy Retro-Futurist Noir With More Energy Than Charm

The premise of the untitled microdrama will revolve around a week of pre-production on a horror movie set. Ian Nsenga and Yuliia Vyshnevska are set to produce. Thom Woodley and Andrew Beck are set as writers, with Gabriel Caste on board to direct.

“Fear Factor” was a hit for NBC in the 2000s. Fox revived the series as “Fear Factor: House of Fear” with host Johnny Knoxville last year. The microdrama edition is a sign of how traditional outlets like Fox and studios such as Endemol Shine are mining their archives for all manner of new iterations. The vertical media boom promises to add a layer of complexity to reaching the mass audiences that the largest media outlets need to be able to support high-end original content.

“ ‘Fear Factor’ has always tapped into something primal: what scares us, how far we’ll go and what happens when we’re forced to confront our fears,” said Hannah Pillemer, head of scripted for Fox Entertainment Studios. [Fox Entertainment president] Michael Thorn and his network team saw the opportunity to bring this iconic franchise to Fox in a bold new way, successfully introducing ‘House of Fear’ and the outrageous Johnny Knoxville to a whole new generation of fans. That tremendous response has opened the door to take ‘Fear Factor somewhere it has never gone before: becoming the first unscripted television franchise to make the leap into original scripted storytelling. Our friends at Endemol Shine came to us with an inspired way to do exactly that, turning everything audiences love about Fear Factor into the foundation for a completely original horror story.”

The fandom for “Fear Factor” has ebbed and flowed over the past decade but it remains a highly visible property for Banijay’s Endemol Shine unit, with some 32 adaptations worldwide.

“We’re grateful to see our vision of turning My Drama into a trusted platform for studios, creators and IP like ‘Fear Factor’ come to life,” said Anatolii Kasianov and Bogdan Nesvit, co-Founders and co-CEOs of Holywater Tech. “This is another step toward expanding the audience and helping vertical TV evolve into mainstream entertainment.”

Michael Heyerman, exec VP of unscripted series for Endemol Shine North America, said “Fear Factor” lends itself to short bursts.

“Vertical storytelling is creating a new language for entertainment, one built on immediacy, intimacy and serialized discovery and ‘Fear Factor’ already speaks it fluently,” Heyerman said. “Our partners at Fox gave the franchise a powerful new life with ‘House of Fear,’ and we’re proud to build on that momentum. Alongside Holywater, together we’re creating a new generation of horror that doesn’t just fit the mobile screen but uses it as part of the experience.”

The series will premiere on My Drama but Fox will handle worldwide distribution and windowing strategies. Fox has been aggressive in developing vertical media properties that can be packaged and sold to linear and streaming platforms around the world, as the company discussed at length earlier this month at the Mipcom content marketplace in Cannes.

#3585. Broadway’s ‘A Few Good Men’, ‘Other Desert Cities’, ‘860’ Fill Seats With Brisk Autumn Box Office

Broadway‘s big-name fall stars continued putting seats in the seats last week, with Billy Crystal‘s 860, Other Desert Cities with Julia Louis-Dreyfus, Ed Harris and Allison Janney, and A Few Good Men with Bradley Whitford and Tom Blythe selling out their venues and pulling seven-figure grosses.

Especially impressive was the rapturously reviewed Other Desert Cities, grossing $2,262,442 for the week ending October 25, second only to Hamilton’s $2,524,951.

860 grossed $1,212,055 in its opening week at the Imperial, a slight downturn in the dollar number only because of press- and opening-night comps. The well-reviewed solo show was SRO with attendance at about 108% of the venue’s capacity. (Earlier today, the production announced a six-week extension to February 14, 2027.) Watch on Deadline

Also opening last week was The Imaginary Invalid starring Bill Irwin, filling about 82% of seats at the non-prof Haimes for a gross of $419,673.

A Few Good Men, the Aaron Sorkin revival in previews at non-prof Lincoln Center Theater’s Vivian Beaumont, grossed $1,371,447. Opening night is this Thursday, October 29.

Another strong newcomer is Wanted, the musical from Angelica Chéri and Ross Baum starring Solea Pfeiffer and Liisi LaFontaine, coming close to selling out the James Earl Jones (93%) for a solid gross of $624,088. Opening night is November 8.

Two shows began previews last week. The controversial Downstate began previews at the Booth, filling 95% of seats and grossing $264,437 for six previews. Opening night is November 15. The reimagined The Fantasticks from non-prof Second Stage began previews at the Hayes, filling the venue for four previews and grossing $254,610. Opening night is November 16.

In addition to 860, A Few Good Men, Other Desert Cities and The Fantasticks, the sell-outs for the week were Hamilton and Oh, Mary! Just shy of selling out were & Juliet, Buena Vista Social Club, Chicago, Hadestown, Harry Potter and the Cursed Child, MJ, Operation Mincemeat, Paranormal Activity, School Girls; Or, The African Mean Girls Play, The Book of Mormon and The Rocky Horror Show.

After Hamilton and Other Desert Cities, the Top Five Earners roster was rounded out by Harry Potter and the Cursed Child ($1,990,388), The Lion King ($1,869,464) and MJ ($1,736,241).

Top average ticket prices went to the three shows averaging more than $200 a seat: Other Desert Cities ($290.35); Hamilton ($235.58); and Oh, Mary! ($213.98). Lowest average ticket price went to Downstate at $61.64. The overall average for the 32 Broadway productions was $129.18.

In all, the 32 productions grossed a total $33,966,016 for the week ending October 25, about even with the previous week but down about 8% from last year at this time. Attendance was 262,928, up 1% from the previous week but down 5% year-to-year.

In the 22nd week of the 2026-27 season, Broadway has grossed $696,438,425 season-to-date, down about 8% from last year at this time, with total attendance of 5,677,469 down 3% year-to-year.

All figures courtesy of The Broadway League. For more box office information visit the League’s website.

#3584. OpenLDK: Bringing the Java World into Common Lisp

Many years ago I worked on an alternate Java compiler and runtime called gcj, the GNU Compiler for the Java Programming Language. These days, my preferred programming language for most tasks is Common Lisp, but I’m still interested in Java runtimes.

Back in late 2024, I wanted to use a Java library from SBCL, but couldn’t find an integration approach that suited the application. This led me to explore the idea of building a simple JVM bytecode processor in Lisp so I could execute the code directly in process.

I started before coding agents became useful for this kind of work, and wrote the first working version by hand.

OpenLDK is a JIT compiler and runtime for Java, written in Common Lisp. It reads Java bytecode, translates it into Lisp, and lets SBCL compile that Lisp into native machine code. Java objects live in the same process and heap as the Lisp application.

My goal is to make Java libraries feel at home in Common Lisp. For general Java execution, HotSpot will be much faster.

In the last few days, however, OpenLDK has gotten much better. I’ve experimented with JVM-hosted languages and Spring Boot application execution.

OpenLDK leads to some wonderfully strange possibilities. Java classes become CLOS classes. Lisp can add methods around methods written in Kotlin. A Clojure function can become something you pass to funcall. Java’s own compiler runs inside SBCL. And an initialized Spring Boot application can be saved in a Lisp image and launched again in about 84 milliseconds.

You can even connect Emacs and SLY to a Spring Boot application running on OpenLDK, inspect its objects, and monkey-patch its behavior while it keeps running.

Here is a tour of how it works, what it can do, and what that startup number actually means.

The compilation pipeline begins with ordinary class files. OpenLDK targets JDK 25 and uses OpenJDK’s runtime libraries, reading them from the JDK’s JMOD files or its lib/modules container.

When a class loads, OpenLDK creates its CLOS representation and installs stubs for its methods. Calling a method for the first time triggers compilation: bytecode becomes an intermediate representation, then Lisp, then native code through SBCL. The compiled definition replaces the stub.

That laziness matters. Loading a library does not mean compiling every method in it. Much of a large library may never be used by a particular application.

Stripped of the generated control-flow scaffolding and exception machinery, its translated body amounts to:

The signed-field operation preserves Java’s 32-bit integer overflow behavior. With typed operands, SBCL can compile this to a machine addition. OpenLDK handles Java’s semantics while drawing on the compiler SBCL already provides.

The translation also makes the program accessible to Lisp. Java fields become slots. Instance methods become methods of CLOS generic functions, identified by their JVM signatures so overloaded methods remain distinct. Static methods become ordinary Lisp functions. Java’s exception hierarchy has a corresponding Lisp condition hierarchy.

Compiled methods are retained in a persistent code cache. Subsequent runs can load cached code when the classes it depends on remain unchanged, and cache entries are separated by OpenLDK build. Different images can reuse compiled JDK methods too.

The most interesting consequences appear when an application starts using these representations directly.

Foreign source can appear directly inside Lisp source. After starting the relevant languages and enabling OpenLDK’s source syntax, a Kotlin expression looks like this:

$n passes the Lisp value into the Kotlin code as an object. Lexical variables, collections, and callbacks can cross the boundary without being serialized into source strings.

The same mechanism supports Scala, Groovy, Clojure, Kawa, JScheme, and ABCL. Each block keeps the language’s own syntax. Clojure also has a #[ ... ] notation.

A Clojure function can come back as a callable foreign-function object. The surrounding Lisp invokes it with ordinary funcall, with argument and result conversion handled by the interop layer. The reverse direction works too: Lisp functions can become callbacks for the other languages.

You can also nest those source blocks. Here is the entire examples/one-form.lisp file, verbatim. After the setup, one expression passes a value through all seven languages:

The $ before a nested block passes its result to the enclosing language. The expression crosses seven language implementations, using their own syntax and libraries, inside one Lisp process. I think that’s extremely cool.

CLOS integration goes further. Lisp can add a method that runs before Java’s ArrayList.clear():

The signature’s V means the method returns Java’s void; size()I returns an integer. This method prints the number of elements before the original implementation clears the list.

Calls from compiled Java, Kotlin, Scala, or Groovy code go through it too.

Java methods participate in CLOS method combination. Lisp can supply :before, :after, and :around behavior at the point where the application needs it.

Lisp can also define a class that Java code sees as an ordinary Java class. OpenLDK’s define-java-class generates a class file and installs it through the application class loader, while its method implementations are Lisp code.

These classes can implement Java interfaces. Their methods can consult Lisp state and call other language components, while Java reflection sees an ordinary Java class.

Error handling participates in the same integration. A NumberFormatException escaping a foreign call becomes a typed Lisp condition, foreign-number-format-error, which is also a parse-error. Other mappings cover arithmetic, file, type, and storage errors.

The foreign-call interface offers use-value and retry restarts. Lisp handlers can supply a replacement value or retry a failed foreign call.

A Lisp error raised inside a callback can travel in the other direction as an appropriate Java exception. If it escapes back to Lisp, the original Lisp condition is recovered. Each side can use its own error-handling facilities.

Collections have similar accommodations. A Java list can be exposed as a Lisp sequence, converting elements as they are accessed. A Lisp sequence or hash table can be presented to Java through a backed list or map view.

The build system joins in as well. ASDF systems can contain Maven JAR components and source components for the supported languages. Kotlin, Scala, and Groovy files compile during ASDF’s compilation operation; their classes become available to later components. Maven components place libraries on the shared classpath. Dependencies must currently be listed explicitly.

Inline Kotlin and Scala blocks can also compile with the surrounding Lisp file when their splices have static types and the language compiler is running. Their class bytes are stored in the FASL. Loading that FASL does not require recompiling those blocks. The interop documentation covers these interfaces and the language-specific conversions.

These features sit on top of some substantial programs already running.

Java’s javac compiler works on OpenLDK, and the build produces a warmed javacl executable. Clojure runs with protocols, records, atoms, and futures. Kawa and JScheme run Scheme. Scala’s compiler and REPL work, Kotlin compiles source into executable class files, and Groovy runs dynamic scripts and statically compiled classes.

ABCL adds a particularly pleasing loop: Common Lisp compiled into Java bytecode, translated back into Common Lisp, then compiled by SBCL. Its REPL, compiler, CLOS, conditions, and Java interop run in that environment.

There are also Spring Boot and Quarkus applications. Spring PetClinic runs with embedded Tomcat, Hibernate, and H2. That is separate from the small command-line application used for the startup measurements below.

All that compilation has a cost. Starting a large language implementation cold can take minutes, because OpenLDK is compiling the implementation itself as it runs. Saving executable images makes a substantial difference.

A warm image runs an application during the build, exercises selected paths, and saves the compiled code. Paths the warmup never exercised still compile on first use.

Application state is a separate choice. By default, dump-warm-image resets application-class static state so initialization can happen again. With :reset-classes nil, it preserves the state left by the warmups. An application designed to reuse that state can begin with much of its initialization already complete.

That is how the Spring Boot snapshot works.

The benchmark application uses auto-configuration, configuration properties bound into a record, dependency injection, and an auto-configured Jackson mapper. A CommandLineRunner produces JSON and reads a value back. Ordinary startup also prints Spring’s banner and logs through Logback.

Its snapshot entry point keeps the initialized context:

With the Spring imports supplied, this is the benchmark’s entry point. The first call starts Spring. Later calls reuse the context and execute its runners.

After loading OpenLDK into SBCL and setting CLASSPATH to the application classes and each required JAR, the image can be built with:

The first warmup initializes Spring; the second exercises the reuse path. Saving the image preserves that initialized object graph.

This is similar in spirit to CRaC (Coordinated Restore at Checkpoint): initialize the application once, save its state, and reuse that work on subsequent launches. CRaC restores a checkpointed JVM process. OpenLDK uses SBCL’s image-saving machinery and calls main again with the Spring context already present. It does not implement the CRaC API or preserve running threads, so thread pools and other process-local resources need attention when designing an application for this approach.

These timings cover process launch through exit for the small command-line application; peak RSS is measured separately. The benchmark script checks that the application output matches across variants. This measures startup and a short task, not steady-state throughput or web-server readiness.

GraalVM is faster and smaller here: 53.5 ms and 41 MB, against OpenLDK’s 83.9 ms and 175 MB. GraalVM still runs Spring initialization at launch; OpenLDK reuses the saved context.

Standard GraalVM Native Image makes a closed-world assumption: all classes must be known at build time, with dynamic accesses covered by analysis or metadata. OpenLDK keeps its class loader and JIT compiler, so new classes and previously unused methods can still compile after launch. Those paths pay their compilation cost when first used.

Saved images also need their classpath files and JDK available at runtime. The image documentation covers deployment constraints.

OpenLDK still has compatibility gaps, including unimplemented bytecode verification. Development targets SBCL on Linux.

#3583. Amazon unveiled 5 new Blink cameras, including a 4K model that caught my eye

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Smart Home > Home Security

Five new Blink cameras are coming to the market, and the extended-range model has all my attention.

2026 has been a year of big upgrades for Amazon’s Blink line of easy-to-install home surveillance cameras, first with 2K resolution and now with 4K and powerful extended-range models that challenge what we can expect from the devices.

Also: HomePod Mini 2 (2026) vs. HomePod Mini (2020): Is Siri AI worth the upgrade?

The five new Blink cameras coming to market are the Blink Frontier XR+, Blink Outdoor 2K XR+, Blink Mini 4K+, Blink Mini Pan-Tilt 2K+, and Blink Spotlight 2K+, filling out its catalog with cameras designed for very large outdoor properties. All the new cameras are available for preorder starting October 21st.

The Blink Mini 4K+ is an $80 wired security camera built for indoor and outdoor use, and the first 4K wired camera from Blink. It can capture a 115-degree field of view, support dual-band Wi-Fi 6, and feature two-way audio.

These features are a big upgrade over the original Blink Mini, the Mini 2, and the newer Mini 2K+. The Blink Mini 4K+ is the category’s flagship this year, finally bringing extraordinary resolution to what was an HD camera just a year ago.

Blink is known for offering inexpensive, easy-to-install security cameras as an alternative to the pricier Ring brand. As a result, these cameras were simplistic and low-resolution. With this latest addition to the lineup, which includes high-resolution and extended-range options, Blink is positioning itself as a major player in the home security market.

Blink is also entering a new category with two extended-range camera models: the rugged Frontier XR+ and the Blink Outdoor 2K XR+. Both are designed to cover large properties where the Sync Module may be up to a couple thousand feet away.

Also: 3 Apple HomeView alternatives you can get today – and two are cheaper

The Frontier XR+ is designed as a trail cam, perfect for remote areas and large properties. This $200 camera comes with the Sync Module XR+ and can reach connectivity to up to 7,000 feet with a clear line of sight. This camera isn’t designed to be used solely as a trail cam, however. It can surveil expansive, distant areas, such as gates or cattle areas.

At $150, the Blink Outdoor 2K XR+ also includes the Sync Module XR+ and can reach up to 2,700 feet with a clear line of sight.

Blink also announced a new Spotlight Cam 2K+ for $110, which is a 2K-resolution camera with four LED spotlights to illuminate dark areas. The camera is battery-operated, making it easy to install wherever you want without wiring or plugging it in.

Even with a new extended-range pair of security cameras, the biggest hardware upgrade is the Blink Mini Pan-Tilt 2K+, which costs $50. Instead of the clunkier pan-tilt Blink Mini from years past, the 2K+ is compact and sleek, with 350-degree pan and 115-degree tilt.

This PTZ camera is also weatherproof, so you can use it outside with a Blink Camera Outdoor Adapter.

#3582. What I Love (and What I Don't) About the New Una Fitness Watch

I’ve reviewed every type of fitness watch and thought I had seen it all, but my first glimpse of an Una watch had my jaw on the floor: There is a USB-C port on the side of the watch. Not a set of pins for a magnetic charger, or a socket that requires a proprietary cable—just a hole you can stick a regular old USB-C cable into. This is what watches have been missing.

The Una watch started popping up on my feed about two years ago, and finally started shipping this summer. I’ve been wearing a review unit for a few weeks. The Una watch is advertised as repairable and upgradable, with open source software. There are little screws that I can see around the watch face, and on the back panel. The Una website sells a replacement battery for $40 and a replacement screen for $50. They also sell the tiny screwdriver that you’ll need to make repairs, but if you’re the kind of person who gets excited about a repairable watch, you may already have your own.

Overall, I like it. The Una watch has an MIP screen (an older tech that is a cult favorite) and an app that's simple but effective. Not everything lived up to my expectations in testing, but no features failed outright. This feels like a solid start to the concept of “one watch for life” and I’m excited to see how the hardware and software offerings evolve over time.

Most wearables these days try to do as many jobs as possible, whether they’re the right tool for those jobs or not. I am honestly fine with a running watch measuring time, distance, and heart rate while I run, and not trying to score my sleep or pretend it can tell how fast I’m aging.

The Una watch app shows my steps, activity, and floors climbed for the day, as well as my average and resting heart rates. When I go for a run, it tracks my pace, distance, and heart rate during the run and gives me a nice page of data afterward. It reliably uploads this data to Strava, and can make little bar graphs of how these numbers vary from day to day.

There is a “store” (everything is free, as far as I can tell) where you can download extra watch faces and features. One I’m trying out is a habit tracker called HabitLab: It asks you every evening whether you had alcohol, cigarettes, a late meal, and so on. Every morning, it asks how you’re feeling. And then, even though the watch doesn’t normally track your sleep, the app takes note of your overnight HRV and resting heart rate. Over time, it will correlate the answers to those questions with the sleep data, to hopefully give results that are similar to Whoop’s journal analysis. (I haven’t been using it long enough to judge how well it works, but stay tuned.)

HabitLab appears in one area of the watch as a standalone app; another, called Gym Sets, gives me a strength training activity type, which the watch did not otherwise have. I just select Gym Sets instead of “Run” or “Workout,” and the app tracks my heart rate while also giving me a set timer.

The store is small, and the apps are simple, without many of the features that you can find on the apps for Apple Watch or other more fully featured devices. But the Una watch has only been out for a few months, so it’s not surprising that users haven’t created a plethora of apps yet. The company’s software development kit, and even its circuit board designs, are free for people to download and develop from.

I was worried that the emphasis on repairability, and the affordable price, meant that the Una watch would be made with components that maybe aren’t fully up to date. Heart rate sensors have been getting better over the years, so Una’s will have to be pretty good if it wants to even sit in the middle of the pack. Garmin, Apple, and now Fitbit have raised the bar quite a bit.

I will say the GPS track was reasonably good; on trail runs and road runs, it accurately followed the path I actually took. (It doesn’t seem to do quite as well as a new Garmin, but it’s still perfectly usable and its distance totals added up to reasonably accurate numbers.)

Heart rate accuracy disappointed me, though. When I first got the watch, the heart rate tracking was abysmal. I sent a few emails to my contact at Una, and almost had to publish this review with very bad news about the watch’s accuracy, but then a firmware update for the Una watch popped up on my phone. I installed it, and then tried another test run or two, and the heart rate accuracy is much better. If you get the watch, make sure it’s updated to at least 1.6.0.

That said, the update brought the heart rate accuracy to a level where I’d say it’s adequate, but it’s not good (and definitely not great). Its graph follows the ups and downs of my heart rate as measured by a chest strap, but sometimes the Una will spike and show a reading that’s significantly higher than my heart is actually beating. In the graph below, note the spike to around 165 when the true value was 155, and then later in the workout the Una watch detects another spike around 160 when my heart rate was actually around 145 and decreasing. (I saw similar anomalies in multiple workouts.)

In my time with the Una watch, many things made me happy. I still love the USB-C charging, and it was a delight to just plug it in to the same nightstand cable I normally use with my phone, no need to go looking for an adapter or dongle.

I like the MIP screen, which was brilliant in the sunlight and had a serviceable backlight for use indoors or in the dark. The small number of workout profiles and the small number of glances and apps is just fine if you’re not asking for much from a watch, and I wasn’t. It’s minimalist, low-distraction, and there isn’t a single AI-based feature anywhere. A true breath of fresh air.

And at $199, you’re not paying extra for these privileges. The price is just a little more than the new Fitbit Edge ($179.99), but less than a Coros Pace 4 ($249) or a Garmin 70 (also $249). The Una watch doesn’t have as many features or activities as these watches, but perhaps those pluses I mentioned make up for it.

The Una watch would be a good pick for someone who is excited about developing apps, wants a repairable watch, always loses charging adapters, or appreciates a minimalist approach. But would I recommend it for the average person who just wants a basic fitness watch? I’m not so sure.

The flaky heart rate sensor disqualifies it for a wearer who wants a more accurate, modern watch. The MIP screen is going to look old and weird to people who have gotten used to brighter, clearer AMOLED screens. The lack of sleep tracking or other convenience metrics may be hard to take when plenty of watches have these features.

So I like this watch today, with reservations, and I have high hopes for the future. If Una lives up to its vision, it’s going to be great to see replaceable components become available—perhaps there will be an option for an AMOLED screen for those who want it, and maybe there’s room for heart rate accuracy improvement in future firmware updates.

But a lot depends on the company itself. “One watch for life” implies that the company stays around long enough to make more than one model. I’m also hopeful that the future offerings will match what people want, and that the watch and app will stay uncluttered and subscription-free. But surely there will be competing interests that the company has to balance as they grow (if they grow), so time will tell whether the reality lives up to the vision. That said, I am liking the vision a lot.
