#3548. These Are Lifehacker's 2026 'Gift of the Year' Award Winners

Maybe this is the year your spouse finally wants to ditch physical books and embrace the e-reader; or maybe you want to help make your parent's home a little smarter. As the gift-giving season approaches, Lifehacker's staff of tech experts and product reviewers has selected our Gift of the Year winners across 15 categories. From smartphones, tablets, and e-readers to fitness trackers, gaming equipment, power tools, and more, these are the items we think are the most gift-worthy in 2026.

And while you're shopping, Lifehacker's sibling brands have also selected unique products for their own Gift of the Year awards. See their best-in-class gift selections for this holiday season at CNET, PCMag, Mashable, ZDNET, and Popular Science.

If you’re buying a smartphone for someone on your list, you have no shortage of options. But for most giftees out there, the iPhone 17e is the best choice. This isn’t the best iPhone, but that’s the point. For a starting price of $699, you get an iPhone with a high-quality display, a solid single rear camera, and Apple’s A19 chip, the second-best processor the company currently makes. You sacrifice some features here, like a high-refresh-rate display, higher-quality cameras, and a faster USB-C port, but those things won’t matter to most. The majority of us want an iPhone that can take great photos, send iMessages, take FaceTime calls, and run our favorite apps. The 17e can do that, without the exorbitant price tags of Apple’s more premium iPhones. If your giftee needs an Android, that’s a different conversation, but for Apple fans, or the platform-agnostic in your life, the 17e makes a great smartphone gift.

Apple’s best iPad for most people is the one simply called the “iPad.” This tablet, sometimes called the “iPad A16” or “iPad (11th generation),” comes with all the features you’d expect from an Apple tablet. It has a solid 11-inch display, Apple’s A16 chip to run iPadOS 27, a camera on the back for scanning documents and light photography, a camera on the front for video calls, and the option to use an Apple Pencil if you wish. It’s compatible with all of the apps on the App Store, and it can handle almost every iPadOS feature Apple makes—minus Apple Intelligence, but that might be a perk for some. While it’s far from Apple’s best iPad, it’s really the best for most people—that’s why it’s our pick for the best tablet to gift.

Our pick for the year's most desirable gift for readers isn't one of Amazon's slick new Kindles, but a tiny, 4.3-inch Chinese import that can pack hundreds of books into a device not much bigger than your library card. The Xteink X4 Pro may have a funny name and a form factor that suggests an e-reader left too long in the dryer, but it's packed with features, including a touchscreen and a temperature-adjustable front light, that belie its $99 list price ($50 less than the cheapest Kindle). Sure, there's a learning curve involved (the native firmware is merely fine, but easily replaced with a crowdsourced alternative developed by a community of online fans), and it won't work with your existing library of Kindle books. But once you've gotten it set up, you'll realize its biggest benefit: It's small enough to fit into any pocket—or even magnetically attach to the back of your phone—so you can easily carry it with you everywhere, and read instead of doomscrolling. After all, the best e-reader is the one you actually use.

Picking headphones for someone can be tricky. Some people love long battery life; others are just looking for whatever pair offers the most features. At the end of the day, price is what sets the best headphones apart from the rest. However, there is one outlier that offers a great value for the money and does well enough in most categories to make most people feel like they're upgrading: the Sony ULT WEAR headphones. At their new low price, there is no other pair of headphones that offers better quality active noise cancellation, app experience, comfort, battery life, and audio quality with LDAC support for Android devices. Not to mention the ULT button, which has two levels of extra bass that will have heads rattling like they're front row at a concert.

If you’re looking to buy a smartwatch for someone in your life and you know they don’t have any strong preferences going in, the Apple Watch SE is likely the best choice for you. It might not be Apple’s most powerful smartwatch, nor does it have the biggest, brightest display, but it does just about everything you’d expect an Apple Watch to do. It pings you with your iPhone notifications, tracks workouts and sleep, and lets you send and receive texts. It has the previous-generation S10 chip, which is plenty of power for watchOS (including Apple Intelligence), and Apple should support it for years to come. You can spend more to get more features on watches like the Series 12 or Ultra 4, but for most, the SE is all the Apple Watch you’ll ever need. And, if your giftee wants the cellular option, the SE can connect to 5G, just like Apple's more expensive watches.

These relatively inexpensive display-style glasses have that Christmas morning "woah" factor: Once the recipient plugs them into any device with USB-C video, they will instantly see a virtual display that's the equivalent of a 147-inch TV at 1080p. They’re particularly good for frequent travelers and dorm-dwellers who can’t agree on what to watch, but really, they're for anyone on your list who likes to play games, watch movies, or use their computer. (So, basically everyone.)

Buying a smart home gift is a tall order. There are so many different categories to choose from, and, within each, there are many considerations to keep in mind, like platform compatibility, feature set, and price. That’s why I think the Amazon Echo Dot Max is the best smart home gift of 2026. It supports a variety of smart home platforms, including Zigbee, Matter, Thread, and Alexa. That way, your giftee can likely connect the Echo Dot Max to most, if not all, of their existing smart home devices, as well as future devices they may pick up. It’s also one of Amazon’s better smart speakers: It isn’t as small as the Echo Dot, so it’s able to fill your room with sound, but it’s also not as expensive as the Echo Studio. It’s a great balance at this price, considering the versatility it offers as a smart home hub.

No single gift will please every gamer, but the Steam Deck comes close. This device blurs all the lines in gaming: It’s a PC that’s the size of a handheld, and it plugs into your television like a console. Fans of gaming on the go will love the portability and the touchscreen. Console gamers will love the friendly user interface, thumbstick controllers, and finally getting the chance to play “PC exclusive” games. PC gamers will love being able to move away from their desks and play video games on the couch or at a bus stop for a change. The Steam Deck is a win-win-win.

You’re probably not going to buy someone a new TV for the holidays, but you can buy the media-hound on your list a gadget to make the TV they already own even better. Enhancing your watching and gaming is the mission of the Philips Hue Play Sync Box. It’s a middleman between a TV and HDMI gear like consoles or streaming boxes that analyzes video signals in real time and tells the connected smart light strip (as well as other compatible smart lights) to match what's on screen. This is great for making gaming more immersive, and it also improves just watching TV by projecting colors behind the set that complement the visuals. The result is the illusion of a larger screen and a very cool special effect.

If you work out at home, investing in some adjustable dumbbells is a no-brainer, and you'd be hard-pressed to find a "best home dumbbells" list without the PowerBlock Elite Adjustable Dumbbells near the top. The futuristic cubic look is stylish, but more importantly, it's a seriously economical, space-saving design—which is the whole point of going adjustable over a traditional rack of individual weights. The weights have flat edges so you can hold them on end on your lap, and unlike a lot of adjustable models, they're compact enough that they won’t bump into each other on shoulder or chest presses.

Fitness trackers come in many forms, including watches, rings, and smart bands. I have favorites in every category, but when it comes to the overall best fitness tracker for most people, the Fitbit Air is the clear winner. It does all the essentials without a subscription, including step tracking, sleep tracking, and automatic workout detection. Its battery lasts a week, and your giftee will have the option to either use the new Google Health app (which I mostly like) or bypass it and use a third-party app like Bevel. More importantly, it's a fraction of the cost of any other tracker out there, so it's a lot more likely to fit your gifting budget than something like an Oura ring.

If you gift a subscription to Bespoke Post, you’re giving the chance to open a different gift every month, like Christmas is lasting all year. Unlike some hyper-niche gift services, Bespoke Post lets users customize their loot boxes: One month, they can choose an outdoorsy vibe, and the next, go for a high-end coffee collection. They can even skip a month if they don’t like what’s on offer. This is an essential gift subscription for anyone buying a present for a hard-to-shop-for dude.

Makita invented cordless drills in 1978, and it has been known for making quality drills ever since. Its new 18-volt LXT combo set is even better than its predecessors because it has a longer battery life with improvements to motor control, overheating, and dust and particulates. The drill has about 70% more power than previous models, allowing you to drill into tough materials like pressure-treated lumber more easily, and the impact driver has a three-speed control, allowing you to adjust the power to match the material you’re driving fasteners into. Since a drill and driver are the foundation of a cordless tool kit, this set, which offers better battery life and more power, stands out—it comes with two batteries, a charger, and a tool bag.

Buying a kitchen gift can be challenging because our kitchens are so personal—some of us know what a braiser is, and some of us keep shoes in our ovens. No matter who you’re shopping for, the Our Place Wonder Oven is the perfect gift. This countertop appliance combines six functions into one stylish unit with a small footprint. If the recipient has a small kitchen, they will love it for its compact nature and for the way it replaces at least three other countertop appliances (a toaster, an air fryer, and a convection oven), clearing space. If the person on your shopping list is an accomplished cook or always busy feeding a family, they’ll love it because it makes preparing fast, casual, and healthy meals a breeze without having to fire up that six-burner behemoth and use 16 pans. It can air-fry, bake, roast, toast, broil, and reheat with aplomb, and it sports an amazing steam infusion feature that makes everything come out moist—even those undated leftovers you pulled out of the freezer in desperation. It also comes with everything you’ll need to start cooking: a wire rack, air fry basket, bake pan, and crumb tray are all included. Did we mention it just looks cool?

A lot of tech might be expensive these days, but that doesn’t mean all of it is. In fact, there are plenty of excellent gadgets you can buy friends and family for $50 or less. Our favorite option in this category is the JBL Go 4. This portable Bluetooth speaker is small but mighty, able to get quite loud for such a tiny device. You’ll get seven hours of playback on one charge, plus an extra two hours with “Playback Boost” enabled. The Go 4 has an IP67 rating against dust and water, so you can bring the speaker with you outdoors without worrying about ruining it. If your friends have JBL speakers of their own, not just Go 4s, you can connect them all together for a quick multi-speaker setup. It really is a versatile device, especially when you consider both its size and its price. The JBL Go 4 makes for a great stocking stuffer, Yankee Swap contribution, or gift for anyone looking for a portable speaker for home or on-the-go use.

#3547. This New Simulation of the Early Universe Is Massive, Detailed and Gorgeous

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The Lumina Project spans 2 billion years and will allow scientists to test theories and predictions about the origins of the cosmos.

The origin of the universe is one of the greatest scientific mysteries of all time. Scientists have developed numerous theories and testable predictions about how the universe began, including the Big Bang model, cosmic inflation and quantum-cosmology proposals.

To test those theories, researchers from the Center for Astrophysics, a collaboration between Harvard and the Smithsonian, assembled the Lumina Project, a simulation of the early universe far larger than any prior one.

The details of the simulation, published in the Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society (PDF), describe how regular matter, dark matter and radiation likely interacted during the first 2 billion years of the universe, covering a region equivalent to a cube roughly 1.5 billion light-years on each side.

For reference, the prior champion for the largest early-universe simulation was the Thesan simulation in 2022, which simulated about a billion years of the early universe across a region roughly equivalent to a 300-million-light-year cube.

It does all of this in incredible detail, largely due to observations made by NASA’s James Webb Space Telescope and other telescopes that spend considerable time observing the oldest celestial objects. By looking at those ancient space objects, scientists have been able to piece together at least some ideas of what the early universe may have looked like.

“With JWST, the early universe has become an exciting new frontier in astrophysics,” said Oliver Zier, lead author of the study, in a statement. “To understand what we are seeing with this telescope, we need to translate our theories into detailed predictions,” Zier said.

The simulation was made on the Frontier supercomputer at Oak Ridge National Laboratory in Tennessee. Frontier has around 37,000 GPUs across its system, and the Lumina Project used about 15,000 of them.

That’s what helped the simulation become the largest and most detailed simulation ever made, giving researchers plenty of unique features to work with.

One example is helium reionization, a process that scientists believe took place in the early universe. Back then, the universe was so hot that virtually all matter was ionized, meaning it consisted of charged particles. When the universe cooled about 380,000 years after the Big Bang, atoms started combining together to make regular hydrogen and helium. Then, a short time later (comparatively speaking), the first generation of stars emerged and emitted enough ultraviolet light to reionize nearby hydrogen and helium atoms.

“Lumina follows both hydrogen and helium reionization,” Zier told CNET in an email. “This allows us to study these processes together in one self-consistent simulation, meaning it captures reionization as it occurred while allowing its effects to immediately influence the evolving universe within the model.”

Aaron Smith, an assistant professor of physics at the University of Texas at Dallas and an author on the paper, said the team is incredibly fortunate to be building simulations like Lumina. “For the first time, we can follow along as both hydrogen and helium changed into their modern form across such an enormous volume of the universe, while still resolving the galaxies and other luminous objects driving that transformation,” Smith said in a statement.

The main function of Lumina is to help scientists test and confirm theories about the early universe and gauge whether their understanding of physics aligns with the simulation’s results.

The team has already used the simulation to make some interesting predictions, including that there were “fewer massive galaxies” in the early universe than James Webb Space Telescope observations suggest. The simulation also houses the mysterious little red dots spotted by JWST, which some scientists now believe are black hole stars.

A side benefit is that the simulation has some truly gorgeous imagery, showing off a lot of interactions of the early universe, including gases at various temperatures mixing together, chemical reactions and the locations of early galaxies and stars. Since the simulation also fills in where dark matter and dark energy would be, the entire thing is a gorgeous litany of colorful clouds, threads and filaments.

Take the above video, for instance. It starts with a wide shot of the entire Lumina simulation volume, then zooms into a single galaxy in virtually empty space, showing how vast this simulation really is. Try pausing the video at almost any point to capture a really cool screenshot.

This next video flies the viewer through a massive halo within the simulation. It starts by adding in various layers, such as dark matter density, gas temperature, gas metallicity (how much heavier elements there are in the cosmic gas, and stellar light. At the end of the video, the layers are turned off, and what you’re left with is a cluster of galaxies.

The Lumina Project has 15 spectacular videos, showcasing everything from gas densities to hydrogen and helium reionization.

#3542. First Commonwealth Reports Tonight: Can Its Community Focus Drive Long-Term Growth?

Picture a local business owner in rural Pennsylvania stepping into a branch office to secure a loan for new equipment. She receives a decision in days, not weeks, from a bank that knows her industry and her community. This is the core of First Commonwealth Financial (FCF -0.80%), a regional holding company providing a full suite of retail and commercial banking services to customers in Pennsylvania and Ohio. The stock recently traded at $21.25 per share, reflecting a 20% gain over the past year as of Sept. 11, 2026.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns First Commonwealth Financial an overall Superscore of 71 out of 100, placing it in the Above Average category. The Superscore is an AI-powered score that evaluates a company's overall strength by combining financial performance, product market position, technological capabilities, leadership quality, and relative valuation. It represents the unification of all our scores into a single score for public companies, with five rating bands: Exceptional (90-100), Strong (75-89), Above Average (60-74), Average (40-59), and Cautious (0-39). This score places the company in the Top ~32% of every company we score, ahead of roughly 68 out of every 100 firms in our database. The Superscore is a single data-driven signal, and this analysis weighs its strengths against the hurdles it faces to help you decide if it fits your own research process.

The company maintains a high level of capital efficiency, meaning it generates significant profit relative to its base of tangible assets. For an income-oriented investor, this efficiency allows the bank to sustain its dividend and capital return programs even when growth is modest.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is one data-driven signal to incorporate into your broader research. Always weigh these metrics against your own financial goals, current portfolio concentration, and personal risk tolerance before making any investment decision.

First Commonwealth Financial is expected to report third-quarter earnings results on Oct. 27. Analysts expect a year-over-year increase in earnings on higher revenue. A beat here could mean a short-term pop for the share price. But long-term investors will want to look past the short-term noise of Q3 earnings.

First Commonwealth's model emphasizes local leadership and personalized banking services for individuals and businesses in Central Ohio and Pennsylvania. That could translate into customer loyalty as well as revenue expansion as the region continues to attract investment in industries including manufacturing, healthcare, construction, and retail. But a bet on this regional bank is also a vote of confidence that its traditional, personalized approach to banking can succeed in a world that is being ever more dominated by artificial intelligence and financial technology that can provide personalized solutions via an algorithm rather than an advisor.

Regional banks are also highly subject to the effects of a potential interest rate hike. First Commonwealth's net interest margin (NIM), which measures how much profit it makes on its lending and investment activities compared to the interest it pays out on deposits and borrowings, was 3.97% for the first six months of 2026, a solid number for a community bank and even higher than its 3.73% NIM in the first half of 2025. This metric will be one to watch when First Commonwealth reports earnings. Its strong customer loyalty may allow it to keep deposit costs relatively low despite an expected rate hike later this year.

#3540. GridCraft is the open-source Excel clone for Linux I’ve been waiting for

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

If spreadsheets are your thing, but Microsoft Office isn't, you might want to give the open-source clone, GridCraft, a try.

ArtCraft, the development team behind PhotoCraft (and a number of other similar apps) reverse-engineered yet another de facto standard app: GridCraft, meant to mimic Microsoft Excel.

ArtCraft’s collection is an important step forward for open source and the Linux operating system because some of the apps they’ve built have long been considered roadblocks to user adoption of Linux. With apps like WordCraft, DeckCraft, and PDFCraft now available, those roadblocks go away. Time will tell, but these apps seem like a big step forward for Linux and open-source to me.

Also: I tried the free Adobe Premiere clone on Linux – and DaVinci Resolve has competition

Although the developers say that these apps are 1:1 replacements for the original apps, that’s not 100% accurate, and my experience with GridCraft confirms that. They are also all in the alpha stages of development, so I wouldn’t advise anyone to use them in a production environment.

You might be thinking that this is simply a rebranded MS Excel that runs on top of an interpretation or abstraction layer, but that is not the case. Like the other ArtCraft apps, GridCraft runs natively on whatever OS you are using (Linux, FreeBSD, MacOS, or Windows). And because these apps were written in Rust, they are blazingly fast.

I’ve opened very large spreadsheets in GridCraft (one with 1,000 rows), and the app never so much as hiccuped.

GridCraft works on more platforms than Excel and is free, which are obvious perks, as is the fact that it’s released under an open license, so anyone can download the code, edit it, rebuild it, and redistribute it.

Also: How to use ChatGPT to write Excel formulas

With GridCraft, you get a standard spreadsheet workflow, native XLSX compatibility, more than 500 calculation functions, charts & pivot tables, agent-native automation, and copy-on-write workbooks with instant undo snapshots, incremental recalculation, and a virtualized grid.

I’ve tested GridCraft on Linux, MacOS, and Windows, and it performs exactly the same and as fast on every platform. So if you’re accustomed to MS Excel’s draggy performance, you’ll be pleasantly surprised with how fast GridCraft does everything. Click on the launcher, and it opens immediately.

Once opened, you can start creating your own spreadsheet from scratch. Although there are three samples (Household Budget, Quarterly Sales Dashboard, and Gradebook), the templates are lacking. You can always create your own templates or download some from the official MS Template download site.

Keep in mind that if there’s one aspect of GridCraft that differs from Excel, it’s templates. I’ve yet to find a way to save a spreadsheet as an .xltx (template) file. Hopefully, the developers will add a more robust template feature in the future. In the meantime, you can always save a spreadsheet as a CSV file into a templates folder you create. It’s a hack, but it works.

As I said, GridCraft is not exactly a 1:1 drop-in replacement for Excel. Even so, it’s pretty close and works as you would expect. It offers the same features, the same UI (minus the MS branding), and does the same calculations. I opened an Excel spreadsheet with a pivot table, and everything was in place and functioning properly.

Although I’ve tested the app on several spreadsheets without a single issue, your spreadsheet could be the one that encounters problems. Because of that, I would suggest always making a copy of your work and using the copy on GridCraft. No matter how well GridCraft performs, do not go into the app thinking it is production-ready.

Also: How I made this great-looking table in Excel

Because spreadsheets can sometimes be the heart of your business or project, you always want to make sure you have a copy of your data. I would say that for any application you use.

Will I be using it for spreadsheets? You bet I will. In fact, I’ve begun adopting all of the ArtCraft apps to replace other, similar apps I use. The one exception to that is FilmCraft, but only because I’ve invested so much time and money into DaVinci Resolve, and FilmCraft doesn’t quite have the color tools and node trees that I’m used to.

As Android Authority reports, Google typically doesn't personalize ads based on content from Drive, Gmail, or Google Photos by default. However, the policy now makes these data available for that purpose if you've connected these apps to other Google services—like Personal Intelligence—that do support personalized ads. Personal Intelligence is the opt-in Gemini feature launched earlier this year that links with other Google apps to give you more tailored, contextual responses in Gemini and Google Search's AI Mode.

Personal Intelligence is opt-in by default, but as Ars Technica points out, users are presented with opportunities to enable this feature and connect apps to it without realizing it or understanding the potential consequences of doing so.

You can check whether any of your Google content apps are connected to Personal Intelligence and disable them if you'd prefer. In your Google Account, tap Data & Privacy. Under Personalization & activity, select Personalization settings, then click Manage Personalized Recommendations in Search Services. Select Connected Content Apps under Additional Personal Intelligence and disconnect any apps you don't want granted access.

#3537. Kevin Bleyer Dies: Emmy Winning ‘Daily Show’ Writer & Barack Obama Speech Writer Killed In Car Crash At 54

Kevin Bleyer, an Emmy- and Peabody Award winning television writer whose credits included The Daily Show and Politically Incorrect with Bill Maher, and who contributed speeches for President Barack Obama including all of the president’s addresses to the annual White House Correspondents Dinners, died in a car accident on Sunday, October 25, on a rural road in Millrift, PA, where he had a country home. He was 54.

“Kevin Bleyer was a writer’s writer,” said his friend, the actor and screenwriter Danny Strong. “From his many years on The Daily Show to writing Obama’s WHCD speeches to his many books and his work on numerous scripted dramas, Kevin could literally do it all. Watch on Deadline

As a speechwriter, Bleyer contributed to many of the speeches of President Barack Obama, including all of the president’s addresses to the annual White House Correspondents Dinners. In scripted episodic television, Kevin was a writer for Disney+’s The Right Stuff — an adaptation of the Tom Wolfe book — Fox’s Sleepy Hollow, and Bravo’s Significant Others.

Bleyer is survived by wife Nella Vera; father Dr. Archie Bleyer; brother Keith Bleyer and sister-in-law Kelly Schulz-Bleyer.

#3536. My iPhone Duo In-Store Experience Almost Has Me Convinced I Need One

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Quite simply, you’ve got to play with Apple’s folding iPhone to really understand its appeal.

Using an iPhone Duo with my own hands turned out to be a remarkably different experience than I imagined.

After watching (too many of) the first YouTube videos and reading reviews last week, I feel like I have a pretty good handle on what the iPhone Duo is: a small iPad Mini, a squat iPhone that transforms into a big iPhone. I appreciated the translucent window animation when you unfold it. I smiled at the interior glow when the alarm went off in tent mode.

Those are all features. I was looking for experience. You can’t get that from watching videos.

To be up front, I’d already decided that the iPhone Duo isn’t the new iPhone for me. As a photographer (and author of a book about iPhone photography), the camera features of the iPhone 18 Pro are objectively superior to what the iPhone Duo offers. And the $2,000 price is a big ask. I’d written off the iPhone Duo as someone else’s luxury.

Now, though, my hands-on time has me wondering if there’s room in my pockets for a Duo.

I spent about 20 minutes with a model at the Apple University Village store in Seattle, so these are first impressions. I defer to my colleague, senior technology reporter Abrar Al-Heeti, who has the most hands-on experience with the iPhone Duo out of anyone at CNET, for her review. “If anything, the Duo’s high cost and limited availability only reinforce that Apple’s foldable isn’t for everyone,” wrote Al-Heeti. “Personally, I have no plans of switching back to a slab phone anytime soon.”

I would go so far as to say that the Duo’s opening effect is everything. It doesn’t need to exist. No one is fooled into thinking the front glass is actually translucent, and if you want to get pedantic, what appears on the inner screen isn’t exactly what you saw on the front screen.

The transition is polished in a way that is quintessentially Apple. There’s a bit of whimsy, the welcome surprise of something you haven’t seen a phone do before and didn’t expect it to do.

The iPhone Duo itself is not necessary. You can buy perfectly capable, affordable phones that do what you already need a phone to do. But that’s not the point. It shows us and other companies that a foldable phone doesn’t have to just be the same phone experience with a bigger screen.

I had to ask an eagerly helpful Apple employee how to do this. With the Duo’s 7.6-inch main screen open, you can set a separate app on each half of the screen.

I expected that a half-swipe from the bottom would trigger it, but it brought up the app switcher interface instead. Instead, you start with the half-swipe and then drag your finger toward the left or right half of the screen. It’s a novel iOS control specifically for the Duo, but it has iPadOS SplitView vibes – just bypassing needing to use the dock or app window traffic light buttons.

I’m sure people will learn it quickly, but it’s still a new gesture in iOS and iPadOS land.

With the iPhone Duo closed, the home screen is like every other iPhone: swiping left lets you view other app screens until you get to the App Library, which puts apps into categories.

On the inner screen, home screen apps hang out on the right side (keeping that translucent glass effect of the opening animation, above), leaving the left side app-free. (Apple’s demo model had a full-screen-size Photos widget in that space.) Swipe left, however, and the apps slide over to make room for the next “screen” of apps, and so on until the App Library drifts in.

The easy design solution would be for the very large screen to just show all the apps when you open the phone. Why waste all the pixels on the left side? Big screen means more stuff!

But that would be disorienting. The object permanence of the apps you were just looking at breaks – do they shift to the left so now you’re looking at different apps? Do other apps take up the space at left, even though they’re not “first-page” apps?

No. Because Apple has set expectations for the experience based on the opening animation.

One thing, in practice, I didn’t catch in videos was how the iPhone Duo handles which screen is used for the camera’s viewfinder. It has four cameras: a selfie camera on the front screen, the main and ultrawide cameras on the back and an under-display selfie camera on the interior.

One great advantage of foldables is being able to open the phone so the front screen shows what the back cameras are seeing – when taking selfies, this means you can use the higher-quality cameras while still seeing how you’re framed. It can also be used as a preview for the subject; you’re taking the photo and looking at the expansive inner screen, while they get to see how you’re framing them in case they have suggestions.

As a photographer, that’s slightly horrifying, because although I’m fine with showing the person some in-progress shots, I don’t want them art-directing me. But I can see how it would be helpful when you’ve asked another person to take your picture; so often I get well-meaning images with acres of space around the edges, and my friends and I as small figures in the middle.

On the iPhone Duo, the front screen displays a Slide to Open control to unlock it for use with the Camera app. It’s reminiscent of the very first iPhone’s unlock mechanism. That way, you’re not accidentally activating the screen.

There’s also a mode control in the top-left corner of the Camera app to specify the role the outer display will play: Duo Preview (described above), Kid Cue (a random Snoopy animation to draw the attention of youngsters who otherwise wouldn’t aim their faces toward the camera long enough for a good shot). There’s also an option to turn the screen off.

My last hands-on impression also centers on experience, but not in the way I’m sure Apple would like. Unlike prior iPhones, the Duo has many different states, which Apple calls “poses.” The accelerometer tracks not only whether the phone is held vertically or horizontally, but also the angle at which the device is folded and positioned.

The phone can be opened and viewed vertically, for example, and then you bend the screen into a 90-degree angle like a laptop – it changes the content, such as playing a movie on the “top” screen and its playback controls on the “bottom” screen.

Perhaps the settings need more tuning, but in the several minutes I had with the phone, it seemed to spend a lot more time spinning its interface from horizontal to vertical to upside down. It wasn’t glitching; it was trying to keep up as I turned, folded, unfolded and used the phone.

The canonical definition of “up” on a phone slips away on a device so adaptable. Even more, you need to use the feel of the hardware buttons (which are in unusual locations compared with current iPhones).

Are customers stricken by iPhone Duo vertigo going to return their devices? Not likely. Because, like the multitasking gesture and the translucent opening effect, they’ll adapt to the new experiences the Duo is delivering.

After 20 minutes of being an iPhone Duo detective, I am genuinely considering getting one. This isn’t without precedent: When the original iPhone arrived, I fawned over the design and features in the same way as I’m doing here, but I didn’t need one, especially since my Palm Treo was a perfectly good smartphone.

Within a few months, the Treo found its new home in a desk drawer, and I’ve owned iPhones ever since. I’m not saying I’m going to place an iPhone Duo order, but I can’t not say that, either.

If you’re wondering about the iPhone Duo’s durability, something I couldn’t really test in 20 minutes, CNET has designed and constructed a custom machine called the Fold-a-Phone that is currently hard at work testing how many times Apple’s foldable can be opened and closed.

#3533. Anthropic trained Claude on illegally obtained Stanford professors' books

AI giant Anthropic illegally scraped and pirated the work of over 10 Stanford professors to train its Claude models, a federal judge ruled in a May 2026 lawsuit.

Anthropic previously trained Claude on illegally obtained texts from professors Tobias Wolff, Abraham Verghese, Chang-Rae Lee, Daniel Mason, Hayagreeva Rao and Myra Strober. In Bartz v. Anthropic, which the Authors Alliance calls the largest copyright settlement involving an AI company, Judge William Alsup ruled that Anthropic could no longer pirate books to create a permanent central library.

While blocking the piracy of work to train AI, Alsup justified the legal acquisition of books to train Claude’s Large Language Models (LLMs) as fair use. He compared AI training to how humans can read, internalize and remember a book’s themes and style. “Like any reader aspiring to be a writer, Anthropic’s LLMs trained upon works not to race ahead and replicate or supplant them — but to turn a hard corner and create something different,” he wrote.

The $1.5 billion settlement went to authors and publishers of nearly 500,000 registered works. Wolff and 27 other authors say the settlement isn’t enough.

“To put me to work that way, without remittance, is a form of slave labor,” said Wolff, professor emeritus of English and winner of the National Medal of Arts. “They’re using my work without paying for it. As time goes on, they’re going to make the lives of writers very difficult if there’s no fruit to their labor.”

10 of Wolff’s publications had been used for AI training without permission.

“[The settlement] still gives Anthropic pretty free rein to roam through our literature and basically take what they think will be useful and profitable for them,” Wolff said.

Wolff, along with English professor Chang-rae Lee, opted out of the Bartz settlement. In May 2026, they filed the separate non-class lawsuit Cruz v. Anthropic, seeking a jury trial for full statutory damages of up to $150,000 per infringed work, surpassing the approximate $3,000 of damages per work offered in the Bartz settlement.

Similar to the claims in Bartz, the plaintiffs allege that Anthropic illegally downloaded, reproduced and distributed their copyrighted books from “shadow libraries” like LibGen and PiLiMi, and that Anthropic broke the law by downloading and storing the pirated files in a central library.

“Our agents didn’t think the blanket lawsuit covered quite enough of the liability to us and are seeking redress in more particular ways,” Wolff said.

If Wolff and his fellow authors are successful, they would gain a permanent injunction against Claude model training using their works.

Paul Goldstein, a Stanford Law leading scholar in intellectual property and one of the plaintiffs represented in the class action, acknowledged the difficulty of addressing and preventing copyright issues for AI companies.

“The essential problem … is what economists call ‘transaction costs,’” Goldstein wrote in an email to The Daily. “Millions of individual copyrighted works may be copied in the course of any training incident, and it’s simply impracticable for the platform to obtain licenses from the copyright owner of every — or, indeed, any — single work.”

Outside of Bartz and Cruz, many authors, visual artists and news outlets, including The New York Times and Getty Images, have filed over 70 infringement lawsuits against AI companies. While Bartz established the current rule for pirating books, the legal future of mass scraping AI models used to train LLMs remains uncertain.

The Authors Guild is also pursuing other lawsuits, including one against OpenAI and Microsoft, as part of a “multi-pronged approach to ensure that the law is clear that AI companies must license the books they use,” according to The Authors Guild Chief Executive Officer Mary Rasenberger.

“Licensing allows authors and publishers to be compensated and to require the use of filters that ensure their works do not appear in outputs unless separately licensed,” Rasenberger wrote in an email to The Daily.

The Authors Guild is working with Congress to create national legislation that will require licensing.

“The Guild is up for the challenge,” Rasenberger wrote. “This is something we have been doing for 114 years, and the moment we are in today, while challenging, also presents opportunities for us to firmly realize our mission.”

For Wolff, this support is critical for the next generation of writers.

“I’m lucky that I’m able to have a life as a writer where I enjoy the profits of my work,” Wolff said. “I worry much more about the young writers I’ve worked with at Stanford and elsewhere being able to do that in the face of these technological challenges, because they’re very hard to grasp and to combat.”

Some authors are waging these difficult legal battles to achieve a sense of credit for their work, outside of the settlement payment.

“Attribution … is very important to the vast majority of authors, and not just academic authors,” Goldstein wrote. “The great majority of countries have something called the ‘moral right of attribution’ on the books to ensure that authors are credited for their work. The U.S., alone among industrialized nations, has no such moral right, which is a terribly unfortunate situation for authors generally.”

As Stanford authors and AI companies continue to navigate copyright challenges for training, both sides must navigate a “foreign land,” Wolff said, meeting at the intersection of creative rights and technological growth.

April Yu ('30) is a Columnist for Arts & Life (vol. 270) and a contributor to the News section of The Daily. She is an intended Human Biology and English major from New Jersey.

#3532. Is SpaceX Stock a Buy After Its Latest Earnings Report?

SpaceX (SPCX +1.14%) posted its third-quarter earnings report on Oct. 20. The aerospace and AI company's revenue surged 161% year over year to $14.6 billion, beating analysts' estimates by $1.6 billion. Its EPS of $0.35 also cleared the consensus forecast by $0.19, marking a major improvement from its net loss of $0.36 per share a year earlier. Those headline numbers were impressive, but is SpaceX's stock worth buying in this choppy market?

In the first nine months of 2026, SpaceX generated 47% of its revenue from its connectivity (Starlink) segment, 40% from its SI ("super intelligence") segment, and 12% from its space segment. On a generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP) basis, Starlink is SpaceX's only profitable business -- and it supports the expansion of its unprofitable space and SI businesses. But if we only look at its adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) -- which generously excludes interest payments on its debt, depreciation on its hardware, and stock-based compensation -- its margins look much better.

Data source: SpaceX. YOY = Year-over-year.

The SI segment -- which houses xAI, Grok, X, Cursor, and its other AI assets -- continues to grow as it secures big infrastructure deals with Anthropic, Alphabet's (GOOG +0.84%) (GOOGL +1.19%) Google, and other big companies. The space segment is growing more slowly, but Starship -- the largest rocket ever built -- should ignite its growth again.

For 2026, analysts expect SpaceX's revenue to surge 161% to $48.8 billion with a GAAP net profit of $3.6 billion -- or $0.21 per share. For 2027, they expect its revenue to jump 264% to $177.6 billion as its net income soars 1,356% to $51.8 billion, or $3.87 per share. Elon Musk claims SpaceX's annual revenue could surpass $1 trillion by 2030.

SpaceX's stock doesn't seem expensive if you expect it to hit those targets. At $150, it trades 11% above its IPO price of $135 and at 38 times next year's earnings. I wouldn't go all-in on SpaceX at these levels yet, but I'd be willing to buy a starter position -- since it clearly has more irons in the fire than many of its smaller competitors in the nascent space industry.

#3531. The latest PowerToys gives your mouse 2 new tricks for free – how to use them

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

PowerToys is one of my favorite free Windows utilities. With more than 30 tools, it offers something for everyone from beginners to power users. Here's what's new for mousing.

Ever have trouble using your mouse? Sometimes selecting a large block of text proves to be a challenge. Other times, your mouse cursor may get in the way of what you’re trying to type or see. Now Microsoft can help you resolve both issues via the latest version of its free PowerToys utility.

Also: 5 apps I always install on new Windows PCs – and most are free

Popping up in the official release of PowerToys version 0.102 are two new tools for you to mouse around with. One is called Mouse Button Lock, while the other is known as CursorHide. I’ll explain how to use each one. But to try them yourself, you’ll first need to install or update PowerToys.

To install the program from scratch, go to the PowerToys Microsoft Store page and click the Download button. Or, you can head to the PowerToys GitHub page and click the correct exe file for your flavor of Windows. To update an existing installation, open PowerToys from the Start menu or the System Tray and click the button to check for updates or select the notice to grab the latest version.

With PowerToys running, select the category for Input & Output on the left and click Mouse Utilities. You can then check out the details on both Mouse Button Lock and CursorHide and turn on the switches for either or both. Now here’s how they work.

Selecting a block of text normally requires that you hold down your left mouse button for the entire process. But if you’re trying to select a long block that extends to multiple sections or pages, that maneuver can be tricky.

With Mouse Button Lock, you’re able to hold down a mouse button for a second or two and then release it. You then move your mouse toward the end of the block you wish to select, and the text is automatically selected along the way. Then just click your mouse button again to stop the selection.

This capability has already been available through the ClickLock setting built into Windows. To access this with or without PowerToys, go to Settings and select Accessibility. Scroll down to the screen to the Interaction section and select the option for Mouse. Click the setting for ClickLock. You can then turn it on and set the duration in seconds to determine how long you must hold down your left mouse button to activate it.

If this functionality is already built into Windows, why enable it in PowerToys? The PowerToys flavor is much more versatile. You can choose the left, right, or middle mouse button as the trigger. You can also set the duration more precisely in miliseconds. Plus, you’re able to choose the distance in pixels during which your selection will remain. On my end, I chose the right mouse button, set the duration to 1,500 ms (1.5 seconds), and increased the distance to 100 pixels.

To try this in action as I set it, move your cursor to the start of the area you want to select and then hold down the right mouse button for around 1.5 seconds. Release the button and then move your mouse to the end of the area you want to select.

Also: How to make Windows more like MacOS

You should see the text being selected along the way. When you’ve reached the end, click the right mouse button again, and the selection will stop. It sounds more intricate than it is. After you try it a couple of times, I think you’ll find it a more convenient way to select a large area of text.

Does the mouse cursor ever get in the way when you’re typing text or just trying to see something on the screen? CursorHide will hide the mouse cursor while you’re typing or after a certain period of inactivity so it doesn’t obscure whatever is on the screen.

For this one, click its entry in the Mouse Utilities section in PowerToys and turn on the switch. You can then choose to hide the cursor during most kinds of keyboard input, after a certain period of cursor inactivity, or both. If you choose cursor inactivity, you’re able to set the delay to determine how long until the cursor disappears, anywhere from 1 to 60 seconds. I enabled both options and set the delay to 5 seconds.

Now open an app, document, or file in which you can type text. Move the mouse so you can see the cursor. As soon as you start typing, it should disappear. Move the mouse to show the cursor again. Next, wait for the number of seconds that you chose for the delay, and the cursor should again vanish. Move the mouse to view it again.

While you’re here, you may want to try some of the other mouse utilities. I like Find My Mouse, which helps you locate a mouse pointer or cursor that’s playing hard to get. I also sometimes use Mouse Highlighter, which visually highlights the area I click on with the mouse.

Also: PowerToys now lets you control your monitor from the taskbar

PowerToys is one of my favorite free Windows utilities. With more than 30 tools, it offers something for everyone from beginners to power users. Take time to explore some of the other tools to see which ones you find helpful and then just try them. You can easily turn each one on and off and use your favorites whenever you want.

#3530. This Roborock Vacuum and Mop Is 28% Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Roborock’s Qrevo 2 Pro robot vacuum and mop is down to $649.98 at Amazon (regularly $899.99)—a $250 discount that brings it to its lowest price ever, according to price trackers—making it a solid mid-range option for homes with a mix of carpets and hard floors. Its 25,000Pa suction is designed to pick up everything from crumbs and dust to pet hair, while the anti-tangle brushes help keep long hair from wrapping around the rollers. More importantly, it automatically detaches its mop heads at the dock before vacuuming carpets, so you don't have to worry about damp rugs as it moves between different floor types.

As for how well it cleans, its mopping performance is excellent, with its maximum water flow and cleaning settings managing to lift even dried-on ketchup, though this mode is slow and leaves floors fairly wet. Vacuuming is generally good, too, especially on carpets. That said, you'll want to consider the clearance beneath your furniture, as the LiDAR sensor mounted on top gives this robot vacuum a relatively tall profile that prevents it from reaching under low furniture.

That limitation aside, much of the day-to-day upkeep is handled by the multifunctional dock, which automatically empties the dustbin, refills the robot’s water tank, washes the mop heads, and dries them with warm air. You'll still need to refill the dock's clean water tank, empty the wastewater, and replace the dust bags periodically, but that's considerably less hands-on work than maintaining a basic robot vacuum and mop. And to give you more control over the Qrevo 2 Pro, the Roborock app lets you map individual rooms, set no-go zones, and schedule targeted cleaning, while support for Alexa, Google Home, and Matter allows you to integrate it into your existing smart home setup.

#3529. Microsoft’s Massive Employee Buyout Shows How AI Is Reshaping the Economy

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The company is offering a voluntary exit to 60,000 eligible employees while betting billions on the AI infrastructure buildout.

Following billions in AI investments last fiscal year, Microsoft announced Monday that it is offering buyouts to roughly 60,000 employees, about a quarter of its workforce, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The voluntary exit offer applies to staff at experience level 70 or below across the US, Canada, India and the UK.

According to Microsoft, the move is tailored to give “eligible employees who may be considering a future outside the company a new choice to make that transition now with support.” Microsoft didn’t say how many employee buyouts it will accept, though it’s likely fewer than those offered the deal. The company didn’t immediately respond to CNET’s request for comment.

The move comes a few months after Microsoft reported it invested about $116 billion in property and equipment — largely for cloud and AI data-center capacity — in the 2026 fiscal year, about $50 billion more compared with the 2025 fiscal year. Microsoft’s operating income increased by about $30 billion between the 2026 and 2025 fiscal years.

In September, Bloomberg reported that Microsoft is planning on tripling its computing power in the coming years, which could result in the company using more energy than all of New York City at peak hours.

The buyout affects teams including Xbox, Windows, Copilot and finance, but excludes certain data-center, sales and marketing staff, according to Bloomberg. Xbox already cut 1,600 employees in July in one round of layoffs, and another round is expected before June 2027. The voluntary exit offers show that Microsoft is prioritizing infrastructure buildout, automation and capital efficiency over hiring.

Employment in AI-exposed roles such as software development and customer experience has fallen by more than 4% this year, while roles that rely on AI automation are showing slower job growth, according to Stanford HAI’s Digital Economy Lab. Though AI might not yet be eliminating jobs at scale, companies are spending billions on compute while becoming far more selective about adding personnel.

#3528. ‘Crystal Lake’ Hits 1.3 Billion Minutes Watched in 11 Days, Becoming Peacock’s No. 4 Scripted Debut Ever

According to Peacock, “Crystal Lake” is its fourth-most-watched scripted original series of all time.

The “Friday the 13th” prequel series was watched for 1.3 billion minutes in its first 11 days of streaming. Divided by the series’ 398-minute runtime, that comes out to a total of 3.3 million estimated views. But in terms of overall minutes watched on Peacock, it ranks behind only “The Best Man: The Final Chapters” (2022), “All Her Fault” (2025) and “The Five Star Weekend” Season 1 (2026). Related Stories Amazon Viking Drama ‘Bloodaxe’ Sets Release Date, Debuts Fierce New Trailer at New York Comic Con 'Club Kid' to Host Free Screenings for Nightlife Communities in 12 U.S. Cities (EXCLUSIVE)

Per Nielsen, “Crystal Lake” ranked as the third-most-watched streaming original series and the third-most-streamed first-season series across all of television during the week of Oct. 19.

The series also boasted strong social engagement, garnering 1.8 billion impressions in one day and more than 7.6 million video views in 11 days, as measured by Sprout Social.

“Crystal Lake” was created by Brad Caleb Kane, who also serves as showrunner. According to the official logline, the series follows “single mother Pam Voorhees (Linda Cardellini) who has been unable to shake her grief after her young, sickly son Jason (Callum Vinson) tragically drowned in the town lake almost a year before. When two strangers arrive at Pam’s door digging into her past, a disturbing chain of events is set into motion, leaving the townspeople of Crystal Lake wondering: Just who is Pam Voorhees?” The cast also includes William Catlett, Devin Kessler, Cameron Scoggins, Gwendolyn Sundstrom, Nick Cordileone, Joy Suprano, Danielle Kotch and Phoenix Parnevik.

#3526. Cristiano Ronaldo warned national team comeback could trigger fresh problems as ex-Portugal boss sets out return conditions

Cristiano Ronaldo’s future with Portugal remains uncertain despite the Portuguese Football Federation (FPF)’s decision to impose a backdated suspension that could allow him to return for November’s Nations League fixtures. As debate continues over his fallout with head coach Jorge Jesus, a former Portugal manager has warned that the next step could prove crucial to preventing another damaging confrontation.

The 41-year-old Al-Nassr forward received a 30-day ban and a €255 ($286) fine after leaving Portugal’s training camp in Copenhagen in September. Although the punishment has largely run its course, Coelho believes the federation must handle the captain’s potential return carefully if it wants to avoid reopening the dispute.

Speaking to Radio Renascença’s Bola Branca, Humberto Coelho backed the decision to suspend Ronaldo but criticized the financial penalty as ineffective. “Ronaldo’s suspension was important, but the fine is a bit out of place and doesn’t help at all,” the former Selecao manager said.

Coelho also argued that the incident should never have escalated to the point where disciplinary action became necessary. “What I know for sure is that if I were the coach, this wouldn’t have happened. That’s for sure. And if I were a player, I don’t think it would have happened either,” he explained.

As a former national team captain himself, Coelho believes the episode reflects a breakdown in responsibility on multiple sides. He described the situation as complicated and expressed concern about the damage it could cause to both Ronaldo and the FPF.

The controversy began during the national team’s September international window, when Ronaldo was an unused substitute in the national team’s 2-1 victory over Norway. The forward later claimed that Jesus had given him assurances about his involvement before deciding against using him.

Ronaldo subsequently left Portugal’s camp on September 30, ahead of the Nations League match against Denmark. In a public statement, he accused Jesus of an “absolute breach of trust,” while the coach denied that the two had made the agreement Ronaldo described.

The FPF disciplinary council eventually found Ronaldo guilty of an offence under Article 160(1) of its disciplinary regulations. On October 19, the federation announced a 30-day suspension and a symbolic monetary fine, taking into account mitigating circumstances that included Ronaldo’s contributions to football and his recent disciplinary record.

Coelho made clear that he would welcome Ronaldo back to Portugal, but stressed that the return must be properly prepared. He believes responsibility cannot rest solely on the player, with the national team coach and federation president also needing to help rebuild trust. “I’d like him to return. The right conditions have to be in place, and that’s up to the coach, the president and Cristiano,” Coelho said.

Pedro Proenca, Cristiano Ronaldo, and Jorge Jesus

He added that the three parties must establish genuine understanding and accept responsibility for their respective roles in resolving the dispute. A return without proper preparation and shared accountability could create further problems rather than settle the existing tensions.

Coelho delivered his strongest warning when discussing what might happen if the situation is mishandled. “If his return isn’t properly planned and prepared, with a strong sense of responsibility from everyone involved, there’s no point in it. Otherwise, serious problems could resurface.”

All eyes are glued to the Portuguese camp to see if CR7 and the manager can finally bury the hatchet and clear a path for the all-time top scorer to return.

With Portugal coach Jorge Jesus still at the center of a controversy surrounding Ronaldo’s international future, the Saudi club’s captain faced another testing evening as he tried to put recent turmoil behind him.

Although the disciplinary proceedings have reached a conclusion, the situation between the two Portuguese soccer figures could still influence what happens next.

The 41-year-old Al-Nassr forward faced punishment for leaving Portugal’s national team camp, but the details of his response have revealed how he attempted to convince officials to consider the full weight of his remarkable career.

#3525. Apple puts its M6 chip in a new iMac and entry-level MacBook Pro

The all-in-one desktop keeps its thin design and seven colours, and its starting price stays at $1,499.

Apple’s new iMac with M6 comes in seven colours

Apple’s latest iMac looks much like the previous one, but the company has packed a new M6 chip into its 24-inch all-in-one desktop, promising faster graphics, more processing power and a substantial boost in on-device AI performance.

The new iMac is available to pre-order from Wednesday in 35 countries, with deliveries and in-store availability starting on November 6.

The familiar design remains intact, including the thin body and seven colour options: blue, green, pink, yellow, purple, orange and silver. Most of the changes are under the hood.

The M6 chip comes with up to 12 CPU cores and 12 GPU cores, two more of each than the M4 in the previous model. Apple claims it delivers the fastest single-core CPU performance of any computer, based on its own tests.

The bigger change is how the chip handles AI. Each GPU core now includes a Neural Accelerator designed to speed up AI workloads, working alongside a 16-core Neural Engine.

Apple says this combination makes on-device AI tasks up to five times faster than on the M4 iMac. Graphics performance in photo editing is up to twice as fast.

Apple’s other performance claims include spreadsheets in Microsoft Excel running up to 1.5 times faster, ray-traced games running up to twice as fast, and storage reading and writing data at up to twice the previous speed.

The company is also targeting people still using older desktops. It says the M6 iMac is up to 18 times faster than the most popular Intel-based iMac.

Compared with the M1 model released in 2021, Apple claims up to 2.5 times the performance in Excel and up to 12 times the on-device AI performance.

The 24-inch 4.5K display is unchanged, with a maximum brightness of 500 nits. Buyers choosing the 12-core configuration can opt for nano-texture glass, which reduces glare.

Connectivity gets a more substantial update as the new iMac supports Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6, while the 12-core version adds 2.5 Gigabit Ethernet and four Thunderbolt 4 ports. It can also drive two external 6K displays.

Moreover, the company has kept the 12-megapixel Center Stage camera, six-speaker sound system and three-microphone array. A matching keyboard and either a mouse or trackpad are included, with the 12-core model getting a keyboard with Touch ID.

“iMac raises the bar yet again,” said Shelly Goldberg, Apple’s vice president of hardware engineering.

The desktop ships with macOS 27, which introduces Apple’s new AI-powered Siri to the Mac. The assistant can answer questions, search through messages, emails and photos, and perform actions across apps.

Siri AI remains in beta, supports six languages, and comes with daily usage limits. Apple plans to offer additional access for a fee later. The assistant first arrived on the iPhone with iOS 27.2. macOS 27 also includes writing tools and a way to organise Safari tabs by topic.

Those prices match what the M4 iMac cost immediately before launch, but they are higher than the original prices for that model.

Apple raised iMac prices by $200 in June as memory and storage costs climbed amid demand from AI data centres, according to EveryMac. Before that increase, the cheapest model cost $1,299.

Apple also said the new iMac’s stand uses 100% recycled aluminium, while its magnets use 100% recycled rare earth elements.

The iMac was not the only Mac to get an update: Apple also unveiled an M6 MacBook Pro. Meanwhile, its new foldable phone has attracted complaints about cracked screens.
